  "text": "[Director 指令] **重新设计 Framework 流程图**\n\n用户反馈当前 fig2_framework 太简单（5 个方块一字排开），要求参考已发表论文的框架图标准重新设计。\n\n**设计要求**：\n\n1. **参考风格**：先 WebSearch \"NeurIPS EMNLP framework figure pipeline diagram\" 找 2-3 个高质量论文框架图作为参考。要求有信息密度、层次感、不是简单方块连线。\n\n2. **内容要求**（信息量要高）：\n   - **左侧：数据构建**：展示 generation chain 的递归结构（seed text → Gen-1 → Gen-2 → ... → Gen-4），用渐变色或递进效果表示深度\n   - **中间：特征提取**：展示 Pythia-410M reference model 提取 NLL 分布，从分布中计算 7 个特征。可以画一个小的 NLL 分布曲线示意图。将 7 features 分成三组（Drifting: ent_mean, TTR, hapax; Intermediate: ent_std, ent_min; Stationary: ent_max, tail_decay）并用颜色编码\n   - **右侧：分类**：XGBoost LOGO → 3-class output (gen-0/gen-1/gen-2+)\n   - **底部或旁边**：DDR 示意——用小的趋势线展示 drifting features 下降 vs stationary features 平稳\n\n3. **视觉要求**：\n   - 双栏宽度（figure*）\n   - 字体 ≥ 7pt\n   - 使用学术论文级配色（不要太花哨，蓝灰色系为主）\n   - 箭头和连线要有层次感\n   - matplotlib 生成 PDF 格式\n\n4. **输出**：\n   - PDF 到 `artifacts/_project/chart/fig2_framework.pdf`（覆盖旧版）\n   - 同步到 `docs/chart/`\n\n用 draw-paper-figure skill 或直接写 matplotlib 代码在 autodl-5090 服务器上执行。",
  "project": "gen-depth-fingerprint"
}

        "content": "Can depth-aware data curation improve downstream language model quality?\nWe fine-tune OLMo-2-0425-1B on five data variants (3 seeds each) and evaluate perplexity on a held-out test set (Table~\\ref{tab:downstream}).\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lrc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Variant} & \\textbf{$n$} & \\textbf{Test PPL} \\\\\n\\midrule\nAll depths            & 12{,}299 & 2.335 $\\pm$ 0.000 \\\\\nSize-matched (random) & 3{,}951  & 2.367 $\\pm$ 0.000 \\\\\nDepth 0--1 only       & 3{,}951  & 2.387 $\\pm$ 0.001 \\\\\nBinary-filtered       & 3{,}951  & 2.389 $\\pm$ 0.000 \\\\\nDepth 0 only          & 797      & 2.681 $\\pm$ 0.001 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Downstream fine-tuning perplexity (OLMo-2-0425-1B, mean $\\pm$ std across 3 seeds). At equal sample count, random subsampling (2.367) outperforms depth-aware curation (depth 0--1: 2.387, binary-filtered: 2.389).}\n\\label{tab:downstream}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe results are unambiguous: data quantity dominates depth composition.\nTraining on all 12{,}299 samples yields the lowest perplexity (2.335).\nAt equal sample count ($n$\\,=\\,3{,}951), random subsampling (2.367) \\emph{outperforms} both depth-stratified selection (depth 0--1: 2.387) and binary detection filtering (2.389).\nCross-seed variance is below 0.002 across all variants, confirming the robustness of this null result.\n\nRestricting training to human-only text (depth 0, $n$\\,=\\,797) produces a sharp perplexity increase to 2.681, driven primarily by the 5$\\times$ reduction in data volume rather than depth composition per se.\n\n\\paragraph{Equivalence testing.}\nWe apply a two one-sided $t$-test (TOST) with a $\\pm$2\\% relative margin (equivalence bounds $\\pm$0.047 on the ppl scale) to formally assess whether the depth-aware and random subsampling conditions produce practically indistinguishable perplexity.\nAt equal sample count ($n$\\,=\\,3{,}951): size-matched (random) ppl\\,=\\,2.367 vs.\\ depth 0--1 ppl\\,=\\,2.387, $\\Delta$\\,=\\,0.020.\nTOST confirms equivalence ($p = 1.1 \\times 10^{-4}$), indicating the two curation strategies yield practically indistinguishable downstream quality within the $\\pm$2\\% margin.\nCross-seed standard deviation is below 0.001 for both variants.\n\nComparing the full dataset ($n$\\,=\\,12{,}299, ppl\\,=\\,2.335) against the depth-filtered subset ($n$\\,=\\,3{,}951, ppl\\,=\\,2.387): TOST \\emph{rejects} equivalence (minimum equivalence margin\\,=\\,0.053, exceeding the $\\pm$2\\% bound), confirming that data quantity drives perplexity reduction beyond what depth-aware selection can recover.\n\nKnowing the generation depth of a document does not, by itself, make it more or less useful for training; at fixed sample count, depth-aware curation is statistically equivalent to uninformed random subsampling.\n\n\\subsection*{Margin Sensitivity Analysis}\n\nTo assess the robustness of the equivalence conclusion to margin choice, we repeat the TOST procedure across multiple equivalence margins \\cite{lakens_equivalence}, following the recommendation to report sensitivity across plausible bounds rather than relying on a single threshold (Table~\\ref{tab:tost-margins}).\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\linewidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{llcc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Comparison} & \\textbf{Margin} & \\textbf{Equiv?} & \\textbf{TOST $p$} \\\\\n\\midrule\nFull (12{,}299) vs.\\ Random (3{,}951) & $\\pm$1\\% & No & 1.0 \\\\\n                                        & $\\pm$2\\% & Yes & $1 \\times 10^{-6}$ \\\\\n                                        & $\\pm$3\\% & Yes & $< 10^{-10}$ \\\\\n                                        & $\\pm$5\\% & Yes & $< 10^{-10}$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nFull (12{,}299) vs.\\ Depth 0--1 (3{,}951) & $\\pm$1\\% & No & 1.0 \\\\\n                                            & $\\pm$2\\% & No & 1.0 \\\\\n                                            & $\\pm$3\\% & Yes & $< 10^{-10}$ \\\\\n                                            & $\\pm$5\\% & Yes & $< 10^{-10}$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\caption{TOST equivalence testing across multiple margins. ``Equiv?'' indicates whether equivalence is established at $\\alpha = 0.05$.}\n\\label{tab:tost-margins}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe full dataset vs.\\ random subsample comparison passes equivalence at the $\\pm$2\\% margin ($p = 10^{-6}$), while the full vs.\\ depth 0--1 comparison requires a $\\pm$3\\% margin.\nIn absolute terms, perplexity differences are small: $\\Delta$\\,=\\,0.032 (full vs.\\ random, 1.4\\% of baseline) and $\\Delta$\\,=\\,0.052 (full vs.\\ depth 0--1, 2.2\\% of baseline).\n\nCohen's $d$ values are 86.0 (full vs.\\ random) and 119.7 (full vs.\\ depth 0--1).\nThese appear large but are unreliable: with only $n$\\,=\\,3 seeds per condition, the pooled standard deviation is extremely small ($<$0.001), artificially inflating $d$.\nThe inflated effect sizes reflect low seed-level variance (confirming reproducibility) rather than practically meaningful perplexity differences.\nThe TOST framework with pre-specified relative margins provides a more appropriate assessment than Cohen's $d$ in this low-seed regime.\n",

$N$\\,=\\,37{,}058) across eight generators from five families (Table~\\ref{tab:logo-results}).\nBecause gen-0 seeds are shared across generators (\\S\\ref{sec:generation}), the 3-class metric may carry upward bias from memorized gen-0 patterns; we therefore also report a leak-free binary metric (gen-1 vs.\\ gen-2+): F1\\,=\\,0.694, CI [0.688, 0.700], which is unaffected by this sharing.\nPer-generator binary F1 ranges from 0.633 (Mistral-7B) to 0.724 (Pythia-2.8B); the primary difficulty is distinguishing gen-1 from gen-2+ (Appendix~\\ref{app:confusion}).\nLarger generators show moderate degradation (Qwen3-8B: 0.692, Mistral-7B: 0.633) relative to the 0.694 average.\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-model generalization.}\nCross-model chains (randomly selected generator per step) yield 3-class F1\\,=\\,0.809 and binary F1\\,=\\,0.764 under 5-fold CV,\\footnote{Cross-model chains use the original five-generator subset (Pythia, OLMo, Qwen); Llama and Mistral were added later for single-generator evaluation.} confirming that depth signal persists when text passes through heterogeneous generators.\nThe higher cross-model performance relative to single-generator LOGO (3-class: 0.809 vs.\\ 0.762) suggests that generator heterogeneity amplifies distributional shifts between depths, making each recursive step more distinct.\nUnder class balancing, macro F1 reaches 0.831 (Table~\\ref{tab:cross-model-perclass}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\footnotesize\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Class} & \\textbf{Prec.} & \\textbf{Rec.} & \\textbf{F1} & \\textbf{95\\% CI} \\\\\n\\midrule\ngen-0   & 0.921 & 0.948 & 0.935 & [.923, .946] \\\\\ngen-1   & 0.737 & 0.803 & 0.768 & [.748, .788] \\\\\ngen-2+  & 0.842 & 0.742 & 0.789 & [.768, .809] \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\textbf{Macro} & --- & --- & \\textbf{0.831} & [.817, .844] \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Cross-model chain per-class performance (class-balanced 5-fold CV, $N$\\,=\\,2{,}964, 5 generators: Pythia-2.8B/1B, OLMo-2-1B, Qwen3-0.6B/1.7B). Macro F1\\,=\\,0.831 under class balancing; under natural class distribution, macro F1\\,=\\,0.809 (as reported in text). Cross-model chains avoid the gen-0 seed sharing issue present in single-generator LOGO.}\n\\label{tab:cross-model-perclass}\n\\end{table}\n\nLeave-one-family-out (LOFO) evaluation yields average binary F1\\,=\\,0.686 ($\\Delta$\\,=\\,$-$0.8\\,pp vs.\\ LOGO), confirming transfer across architectural families under the strictest unseen-generator test.\nPer-family LOGO performance reveals a scale effect: Pythia generators (1B, 2.8B) achieve the highest binary F1 (0.718--0.724), followed by Qwen (0.691--0.709) and OLMo (0.695), while larger generators Qwen3-8B (0.692) and Mistral-7B (0.633) show degradation.\nThis pattern is consistent with higher-capacity models generating text closer to the human baseline distribution, compressing the feature margins that DDR exploits.\nAppendix~\\ref{app:domain} further demonstrates that the signal generalizes across text domains, though with substantial variation (Wikipedia binary F1\\,=\\,0.789 vs.\\ Academic 0.628 at $t{=}0.7$; all domains $>$\\,0.73 at $t{=}1.0$).\n\n\\paragraph{Length confound control.}\nLength-matched subsampling yields binary F1\\,=\\,0.724 (vs.\\ 0.770 unmatched; $\\Delta$\\,=\\,$-$0.046); a random-subsample control at equal $N$ (F1\\,=\\,0.767) confirms the drop reflects deconfounding, not sample-size reduction.\nLength accounts for $\\approx$6\\% of discriminative performance (Appendix~\\ref{app:length}).\n\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\footnotesize\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{llcccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Model} & \\textbf{Family} & \\textbf{Params} & \\textbf{3-cl F1} & \\textbf{Bin F1} & \\textbf{95\\% CI} \\\\\n\\midrule\nPythia-2.8B     & Pythia  & 2.8B & 0.798 & \\textbf{0.724} & [.707, .742] \\\\\nPythia-1B       & Pythia  & 1.0B & \\textbf{0.806} & 0.718 & [.700, .735] \\\\\nQwen3-1.7B     & Qwen    & 1.7B & 0.772 & 0.709 & [.693, .726] \\\\\nLlama-3.2-3B   & Llama   & 3.2B & 0.775 & 0.697 & [.680, .713] \\\\\nOLMo-2-1B      & OLMo    & 1.5B & 0.781 & 0.695 & [.679, .711] \\\\\nQwen3-8B       & Qwen    & 8.2B & 0.714 & 0.692 & [.674, .708] \\\\\nQwen3-0.6B     & Qwen    & 0.6B & 0.780 & 0.691 & [.673, .709] \\\\\nMistral-7B     & Mistral & 7.0B & 0.682 & 0.633 & [.617, .648] \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{3}{l}{\\textbf{Average (8-gen LOGO)}} & \\textbf{0.762} & \\textbf{0.694} & [.688, .700] \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\caption{8-generator LOGO macro-F1 for the 7-feature ensemble (bootstrap 95\\% CIs, $n$\\,=\\,1000). Binary F1 (gen-1 vs.\\ gen-2+) is the primary metric. Note: 3-class F1 may carry upward bias from gen-0 seed sharing across generators; binary F1 is leak-free. Cross-model chains (Table~\\ref{tab:cross-ref}) use sample-level CV without this issue.}\n\\label{tab:logo-results}\n\\end{table}\n\nOrdinal regression methods (CORAL \\cite{coral_ordinal}, Frank-Hall \\cite{frank_hall_ordinal}) achieve comparable F1 with overlapping CIs, confirming that 3-class granularity provides insufficient resolution for ranking-aware losses.\n\n\\subsection{Differential Depth Response}\n\\label{sec:results-differential}\n\nFeatures exhibit \\emph{differential depth response} (DDR): central-tendency and diversity measures drift monotonically with depth, while shape measures remain near-stationary, consistent with recursive generation inducing a location shift in the NLL distribution while preserving its functional form.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig1_differential_response.pdf}\n\\caption{Differential depth response: Spearman correlations between individual features and generation depth. Central-tendency and diversity features (hapax ratio, TTR, entropy mean) show substantial monotonic drift; entropy std ($|\\rho|{=}0.17$) occupies an intermediate position; shape features (entropy max, tail decay) remain near-stationary ($|\\rho| < 0.10$).}\n\\label{fig:feature-drift}\n\\end{figure}\n\nPooled class-balanced Spearman correlations with depth confirm the split (Figure~\\ref{fig:feature-drift}):\ndrifting features show substantial monotonic trends (hapax ratio $\\rho = -0.63$, TTR $\\rho = -0.61$, entropy mean $\\rho = -0.60$),\nwhile shape features remain near-stationary (tail decay $|\\rho| = 0.04$); entropy std occupies an intermediate position ($|\\rho| = 0.17$).\n\n\\paragraph{Distributional shift quantification.}\nTwo-sample Kolmogorov--Smirnov tests between gen-1 and gen-2+ distributions isolate the recursive depth effect (excluding the initial human$\\rightarrow$LLM transition): drifting features exhibit large distributional shifts (entropy mean KS\\,=\\,0.537, TTR\\,=\\,0.517, hapax ratio\\,=\\,0.516; all $p \\approx 0$), while shape features show order-of-magnitude smaller shifts (entropy max KS\\,=\\,0.043, tail decay\\,=\\,0.052).\nAll seven features reach statistical significance after Bonferroni correction, but shape-feature KS statistics are ${\\sim}10\\times$ smaller than drifting features, supporting the ``approximately stationary'' characterization.\nEntropy std occupies an intermediate position (KS\\,=\\,0.285), consistent with its borderline rank correlation.\nLevene tests for variance homogeneity reject equal variance for all seven features ($p < 10^{-10}$), indicating that recursive generation alters spread as well as location, though the magnitude of variance change is substantially smaller for shape features (coefficient of variation shift $<$3\\% for entropy max vs.\\ $>$15\\% for hapax ratio).\n\n\\paragraph{Feature complementarity.}\nUnder 10-fold stratified CV, the full 7-feature set (F1\\,=\\,0.773) significantly outperforms surface-only (F1\\,=\\,0.688) and reference-only (F1\\,=\\,0.713; McNemar $p \\approx 0$; Table~\\ref{tab:ablation}).\nReference-only features yield higher binary F1 (0.833 vs.\\ 0.695 for the full ensemble) because surface lexical features introduce noise at the gen-1/gen-2+ boundary.\nHowever, surface features are essential for 3-class classification, where they improve gen-0 discrimination by capturing vocabulary diversity patterns absent in reference-model entropy statistics (Full 3-class F1\\,=\\,0.773 vs.\\ Reference-only 0.713).\nPermutation importance: hapax ratio (0.35), entropy mean (0.23), TTR (0.18).\nLeave-one-feature-out confirms 6 of 7 features contribute positively (Appendix~\\ref{app:lofo}).\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-family reference models.}\nAlternative references (OLMo-2-1B, Qwen3-0.6B) achieve comparable LOGO F1 (spread 0.013; Table~\\ref{tab:cross-ref}), with consistently negative entropy drift, confirming DDR is a property of recursive generation.\n\n\\paragraph{Temperature robustness.}\nThe method operates reliably within $t \\in [0.5, 0.9]$: F1 varies by at most 1\\,pp from the default $t{=}0.7$ across this range (Appendix~\\ref{app:sampling-sensitivity}).\nAt $t{=}1.0$, performance degrades by ${\\sim}6$\\,pp as the primary depth signal collapses: entropy mean's Spearman correlation with depth drops from $\\rho = -0.60$ to $\\rho = -0.002$, indicating that high-temperature sampling injects sufficient token-level randomness to mask the systematic location shift that DDR exploits.\n\n\\input{table4}\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\footnotesize\n\\begin{tabular}{lcc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Feature set} & \\textbf{F1} & \\textbf{Top feature (imp.)} \\\\\n\\midrule\nFull (7 features)     & 0.773 & hapax\\_ratio (0.35) \\\\\nSurface only (2)      & 0.688 & hapax\\_ratio (0.41) \\\\\nReference only (5)    & 0.713 & entropy\\_mean (0.27) \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Feature ablation (8-gen, 10-fold stratified CV, 3-class). Full $>$ surface (+8.5\\,pp) and Full $>$ reference (+6.0\\,pp) confirms complementarity.}\n\\label{tab:ablation}\n\\end{table}\n\n\n\\subsection{Comparison with Detection Baselines}\n\\label{sec:results-baselines}\n\nCan existing detection methods, designed for binary classification, also discriminate generation depth?\nWe repurpose four detection scores as ordinal depth predictors using the same XGBoost framework and identical LOGO splits (Table~\\ref{tab:baselines}, Figure~\\ref{fig:baseline-ci}).\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig3_baseline_comparison.png}\n\\caption{LOGO binary F1 with 95\\% bootstrap CIs. Per-generator variance is higher for single detectors than for the ensemble.}\n\\label{fig:baseline-ci}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\footnotesize\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Method} & \\textbf{Feat.} & \\textbf{3-cl F1} & \\textbf{Bin F1} & \\textbf{ms/text} \\\\\n\\midrule\n4-det.\\ + surface     & 6 & \\textbf{0.795} & \\textbf{0.708} & 22.8 \\\\\n4-detector ens.\\      & 4 & 0.785 & 0.700 & 22.8 \\\\\n7-feature (ours)      & 7 & 0.774 & 0.705 & \\textbf{9.7} \\\\\nAvg-LogProb           & 1 & 0.707 & 0.673 & --- \\\\\nFastDetectGPT         & 1 & 0.660 & 0.569 & --- \\\\\nLD-Score              & 1 & 0.630 & 0.675 & --- \\\\\nBinoculars            & 1 & 0.411 & 0.539 & --- \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Depth classification (8-gen pooled LOGO). All methods use XGBoost with $n_\\text{estimators}{=}200$, $\\max_\\text{depth}{=}6$. Binary: gen-1 vs.\\ gen-2+. Inference cost: 7-feature requires one 410M reference model (905\\,MB); 4-det ensembles require two models totaling 1.4B parameters (2{,}868\\,MB).}\n\\label{tab:baselines}\n\\end{table}\n\nOur 7-feature ensemble significantly outperforms all individual detection baselines (Avg-LogProb, FastDetectGPT, Binoculars, LD-Score) by 6--11\\,pp in 3-class F1.\nThe multi-detector ensembles (4-det, 4-det+surface) achieve moderately higher 3-class F1 (0.785--0.795 vs.\\ 0.774), while binary performance is statistically indistinguishable ($\\leq$0.3\\,pp).\nCritically, our approach requires only a single lightweight reference model (Pythia-410M, 410M parameters) with 2.4$\\times$ faster inference and 3.2$\\times$ lower GPU memory than the best multi-detector ensemble, which requires two models totaling 1.4B parameters.\n\n\n\\subsection{Downstream Utility}\n\\label{sec:results-downstream}\n\nIf depth fingerprinting reliably identifies recursive text, can it improve data curation?\nFine-tuning OLMo-2-0425-1B on five data variants (3 seeds each) yields two conclusions.\nAt equal sample count ($n$\\,=\\,3{,}951), depth-aware selection is practically equivalent to random subsampling (ppl 2.387 vs.\\ 2.367; TOST $\\pm$2\\% relative margin, $p = 1.1 \\times 10^{-4}$).\nHowever, data quantity dominates: the full dataset ($n$\\,=\\,12{,}299, ppl\\,=\\,2.335) yields significantly lower perplexity than any depth-filtered subset, and TOST rejects equivalence at the same margin (Appendix~\\ref{app:downstream}).\nDepth composition per se does not determine training utility at fixed sample count.\n",
        "numLines": 172,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 172
      }
    },

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01AztLeBXiQsEf1mfFxFxxxi`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Discussion}
2	\label{sec:discussion}
3	
4	\paragraph{Why does shape remain approximately stationary?}
5	The softmax architecture and sampling hyperparameters that determine distributional shape are fixed across depths; recursive generation shifts context toward high-probability regions but does not alter the generation mechanism itself.
6	This predicts approximate stationarity under standard decoding, confirmed by sampling ablation ($t \in [0.5, 0.7]$: ${\leq}0.3$\,pp degradation; $t = 1.0$: $-6$\,pp; Appendix~\ref{app:sampling-sensitivity}), and implies the phenomenon reflects a tendency of standard decoding rather than an architectural guarantee.
7	Entropy std ($|\rho| = 0.17$) is a borderline case between the drifting central-tendency features ($|\rho| > 0.60$) and the near-stationary tail decay ($|\rho| = 0.04$), consistent with a feature that partially captures location-dependent variance.
8	
9	\paragraph{Prompt-type dependence.}
10	Semantic-preservation constraints attenuate drift (elaboration F1\,=\,0.730 vs.\ continuation 0.781; Appendix~\ref{app:prompt-type}), consistent with \citet{broken_telephone}: the more a task anchors the output distribution to the input, the less room recursive drift has to accumulate.
11	Cross-type transfer remains viable (F1\,=\,0.753).
12	
13	\paragraph{Practical applicability.}
14	Document-level gen-1 per-class F1\,=\,0.63 is insufficient for automated filtering but sufficient for corpus-level flagging, where error scales as $O(1/\sqrt{n})$.
15	Misclassifications concentrate in shallow gen-2+ samples where drift has not yet accumulated (Appendix~\ref{app:error-analysis}), consistent with DDR predictions.
16	
17	\paragraph{The gen-1\,/\,gen-2+ boundary.}
18	The primary classification difficulty is distinguishing gen-1 from gen-2+: 86\% of binary errors are false negatives (gen-2+ predicted as gen-1), with depth-2 error rate at 48\% compared to 20\% for depth-4.
19	This asymmetry follows directly from DDR: the first recursive step produces the smallest location shift, so depth-2 text occupies a region of feature space that overlaps heavily with gen-1.
20	Larger generators exacerbate the overlap (Mistral-7B error rate 45\% vs.\ Pythia-1B 18\%), consistent with higher-capacity models producing text that more closely matches the statistical properties of human-sourced input.
21	
22	\paragraph{Relation to model collapse.}
23	Model collapse \cite{curse_of_recursion,strong_model_collapse} describes degradation when models are \emph{retrained} on their own outputs, progressively losing distributional coverage through parameter updates.
24	DDR operates in a fundamentally different regime: inference-chain drift occurs with fixed parameters, and is fully reversible by changing the seed text.
25	The two phenomena also diverge in their distributional signatures: model collapse predicts progressive tail shrinkage as minority modes are lost from the training distribution \cite{curse_of_recursion}, whereas DDR preserves distributional shape (entropy max, tail decay remain stationary) while shifting only the location.
26	This suggests that inference-chain drift reflects compression \emph{within} the model's existing capacity rather than \emph{erosion} of that capacity, and may represent a pre-collapse regime where distributional narrowing is detectable but not yet irreversible.
27	Topic drift shows weak correlation with depth features ($|\rho| < 0.29$; embedding similarity between successive generations correlates with entropy drift at $\rho = -0.286$), confirming it mediates rather than confounds depth signal.
28	
29	\paragraph{Granularity and robustness.}
30	Three classes represent the practical resolution ceiling: 5-class LOGO F1 drops to $\approx$0.50, with depths 2--4 mutually indistinguishable (Appendix~\ref{app:finer-depth}).
31	That said, QLoRA fine-tuning recovers 3-class F1\,=\,0.905 (Appendix~\ref{app:qlora}), confirming that richer signal exists beyond the seven summary statistics, though at the cost of interpretability and the DDR decomposition that is a core contribution of this work.
32	Under text perturbation, LLM-simulated sentence regeneration (50\% edit distance) causes comparable degradation to 5\% random word replacement (Appendix~\ref{app:human-edit}), confirming that depth signal resides in generative structure rather than surface token statistics.
33	
34	\paragraph{Instruction tuning.}
35	Instruction-tuned models show substantially weaker fingerprints (OLMo-Instruct LOGO F1\,=\,0.338 vs.\ 0.763 base).
36	Length regulation and format constraints imposed by alignment compress the distributional variation that depth fingerprinting exploits, justifying our restriction to base models as the appropriate setting for isolating the phenomenon.
37	
```

```text
1	This appendix provides detailed visualizations of the feature distributions across generation depths and the resulting embedding structure.
2	
3	\subsection*{Per-Feature Violin Plots}
4	
5	Figure~\ref{fig:violin} shows the full distribution of each feature across five generation depths (0--4), pooled across all eight generators ($N$\,=\,37{,}058).
6	The violin plots confirm the DDR pattern identified in the main text: drifting features (entropy mean, TTR, hapax ratio) show progressive narrowing and location shift, while shape features (entropy max, tail decay) maintain approximately constant distributions across depths.
7	Entropy std exhibits intermediate behavior with modest location shift.
8	
9	\begin{figure*}[ht]
10	\centering
11	\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{fig_appendix_violin.pdf}
12	\caption{Feature distributions by generation depth (violin plots, $N$\,=\,37{,}058). Drifting features (entropy mean, TTR, hapax ratio: $|\rho| > 0.60$) show clear location shifts and narrowing with increasing depth. Shape features (entropy max, tail decay: $|\rho| < 0.10$) remain approximately stationary. Quartile lines within each violin indicate median and interquartile range.}
13	\label{fig:violin}
14	\end{figure*}
15	
16	\subsection*{DDR Trend Lines}
17	
18	Figure~\ref{fig:ddr-trends} shows the per-feature depth trend (mean across all samples at each depth) with individual generator traces overlaid.
19	The consistent downward trajectories for drifting features across all generators confirm that DDR is a universal property of recursive generation, not specific to any single model.
20	Shape features exhibit flat trajectories regardless of generator, with near-zero $R^2$ for the linear fit.
21	
22	\begin{figure*}[ht]
23	\centering
24	\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{fig_appendix_ddr_trends.pdf}
25	\caption{Per-feature depth trends. Black line: pooled mean ($\pm$1 std shading) across all generators. Colored traces: individual generators. Features are labeled by DDR category (drifting, intermediate, stationary). Spearman $\rho$ and linear $R^2$ are annotated per panel.}
26	\label{fig:ddr-trends}
27	\end{figure*}
28	
29	\subsection*{UMAP Embedding}
30	
31	Figure~\ref{fig:umap} shows a 2D UMAP projection of the 7-feature space (7{,}500 stratified samples, StandardScaler normalization, $k$\,=\,30 neighbors, min\_dist\,=\,0.3).
32	Panel (a) reveals a depth gradient from gen-0 (green) through gen-4 (yellow), with progressive overlap at deeper generations consistent with the convergence that motivates the gen-2+ class collapse.
33	Panel (b) shows the 3-class view: gen-0 forms a separable cluster, while gen-1 and gen-2+ overlap substantially in feature space, explaining the classification difficulty at this boundary.
34	
35	\begin{figure*}[ht]
36	\centering
37	\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{fig_appendix_umap.pdf}
38	\caption{UMAP 2D projection of the 7-feature space ($N$\,=\,7{,}500 stratified sample). (a) Colored by raw generation depth (0--4): a gradient structure emerges, with deeper depths converging. (b) Colored by 3-class label: gen-0 separates clearly; gen-1 and gen-2+ overlap, consistent with the high false-negative rate at the gen-1/gen-2+ boundary.}
39	\label{fig:umap}
40	\end{figure*}
41	
42	\subsection*{Confusion Matrix Visualization}
43	
44	Figure~\ref{fig:confusion-heatmap} provides a heatmap visualization of the 3-class LOGO confusion matrix (complementing Table~\ref{tab:confusion} in Appendix~\ref{app:confusion}).
45	The row-normalized view in panel (b) highlights: (1) near-perfect gen-0 classification (99.4\% recall), (2) gen-1 confusion primarily with gen-2+ (18.8\%) rather than gen-0 (6.2\%), and (3) gen-2+ misclassification predominantly into gen-1 (23.3\%), consistent with DDR predictions that shallow recursive text has weak fingerprints.
46	
47	\begin{figure*}[ht]
48	\centering
49	\includegraphics[width=0.85\textwidth]{fig_appendix_confusion.pdf}
50	\caption{3-class LOGO confusion matrix ($N$\,=\,27{,}529). (a) Raw counts. (b) Row-normalized recall percentages. Gen-0 achieves near-perfect classification; the primary confusion axis is gen-1 $\leftrightarrow$ gen-2+.}
51	\label{fig:confusion-heatmap}
52	\end{figure*}
53	
54	\subsection*{Per-Generator Performance}
55	
56	Figure~\ref{fig:per-gen} visualizes per-generator LOGO performance.
57	Panel (a) shows the 8-generator ranking: Pythia models (1B, 2.8B) achieve highest F1, while larger models (Mistral-7B, Qwen3-8B) show degradation.
58	Dashed lines indicate pool-average performance.
59	The scale effect (larger models $\rightarrow$ lower F1) is consistent with higher-capacity models generating text statistically closer to human writing.
60	
61	\begin{figure*}[ht]
62	\centering
63	\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{fig_appendix_per_gen.pdf}
64	\caption{Per-generator LOGO performance. (a) 8-generator binary and 3-class F1, sorted by binary F1. Dashed lines: pool average. (b) 6-generator LOGO detail heatmap showing the performance spread across model families.}
65	\label{fig:per-gen}
66	\end{figure*}
67	
```

```text
1	\section{Results}
2	\label{sec:results}
3	
4	\subsection{Depth Classification Performance}
5	\label{sec:results-classification}
6	
7	The 7-feature ensemble achieves 3-class LOGO F1\,=\,0.762 (95\% CI [0.758, 0.766]; $N$\,=\,37{,}058) across eight generators from five families (Table~\ref{tab:logo-results}).
8	Because gen-0 seeds are shared across generators (\S\ref{sec:generation}), the 3-class metric may carry upward bias from memorized gen-0 patterns; we therefore also report a leak-free binary metric (gen-1 vs.\ gen-2+): F1\,=\,0.694, CI [0.688, 0.700], which is unaffected by this sharing.
9	Per-generator binary F1 ranges from 0.633 (Mistral-7B) to 0.724 (Pythia-2.8B); the primary difficulty is distinguishing gen-1 from gen-2+ (Appendix~\ref{app:confusion}).
10	Larger generators show moderate degradation (Qwen3-8B: 0.692, Mistral-7B: 0.633) relative to the 0.694 average.
11	
12	\paragraph{Cross-model generalization.}
13	Cross-model chains (randomly selected generator per step) yield 3-class F1\,=\,0.809 and binary F1\,=\,0.764 under 5-fold CV,\footnote{Cross-model chains use the original five-generator subset (Pythia, OLMo, Qwen); Llama and Mistral were added later for single-generator evaluation.} confirming that depth signal persists when text passes through heterogeneous generators.
14	The higher cross-model performance relative to single-generator LOGO (3-class: 0.809 vs.\ 0.762) suggests that generator heterogeneity amplifies distributional shifts between depths, making each recursive step more distinct.
15	Under class balancing, macro F1 reaches 0.831 (Table~\ref{tab:cross-model-perclass}).
16	
17	\begin{table}[t]
18	\centering
19	\footnotesize
20	\begin{tabular}{lcccc}
21	\toprule
22	\textbf{Class} & \textbf{Prec.} & \textbf{Rec.} & \textbf{F1} & \textbf{95\% CI} \\
23	\midrule
24	gen-0   & 0.921 & 0.948 & 0.935 & [.923, .946] \\
25	gen-1   & 0.737 & 0.803 & 0.768 & [.748, .788] \\
26	gen-2+  & 0.842 & 0.742 & 0.789 & [.768, .809] \\
27	\midrule
28	\textbf{Macro} & --- & --- & \textbf{0.831} & [.817, .844] \\
29	\bottomrule
30	\end{tabular}
31	\caption{Cross-model chain per-class performance (class-balanced 5-fold CV, $N$\,=\,2{,}964, 5 generators: Pythia-2.8B/1B, OLMo-2-1B, Qwen3-0.6B/1.7B). Macro F1\,=\,0.831 under class balancing; under natural class distribution, macro F1\,=\,0.809 (as reported in text). Cross-model chains avoid the gen-0 seed sharing issue present in single-generator LOGO.}
32	\label{tab:cross-model-perclass}
33	\end{table}
34	
35	Leave-one-family-out (LOFO) evaluation yields average binary F1\,=\,0.686 ($\Delta$\,=\,$-$0.8\,pp vs.\ LOGO), confirming transfer across architectural families under the strictest unseen-generator test.
36	Per-family LOGO performance reveals a scale effect: Pythia generators (1B, 2.8B) achieve the highest binary F1 (0.718--0.724), followed by Qwen (0.691--0.709) and OLMo (0.695), while larger generators Qwen3-8B (0.692) and Mistral-7B (0.633) show degradation.
37	This pattern is consistent with higher-capacity models generating text closer to the human baseline distribution, compressing the feature margins that DDR exploits.
38	Appendix~\ref{app:domain} further demonstrates that the signal generalizes across text domains, though with substantial variation (Wikipedia binary F1\,=\,0.789 vs.\ Academic 0.628 at $t{=}0.7$; all domains $>$\,0.73 at $t{=}1.0$).
39	
40	\paragraph{Length confound control.}
41	Length-matched subsampling yields binary F1\,=\,0.724 (vs.\ 0.770 unmatched; $\Delta$\,=\,$-$0.046); a random-subsample control at equal $N$ (F1\,=\,0.767) confirms the drop reflects deconfounding, not sample-size reduction.
42	Length accounts for $\approx$6\% of discriminative performance (Appendix~\ref{app:length}).
43

Note: 3-class F1 may carry upward bias from gen-0 seed sharing across generators; binary F1 is leak-free. Cross-model chains (Table~\ref{tab:cross-ref}) use sample-level CV without this issue.}

68	
69	Ordinal regression methods (CORAL \cite{coral_ordinal}, Frank-Hall \cite{frank_hall_ordinal}) achieve comparable F1 with overlapping CIs, confirming that 3-class granularity provides insufficient resolution for ranking-aware losses.
70	
71	\subsection{Differential Depth Response}
72	\label{sec:results-differential}
73	
74	Features exhibit \emph{differential depth response} (DDR): central-tendency and diversity measures drift monotonically with depth, while shape measures remain near-stationary, consistent with recursive generation inducing a location shift in the NLL distribution while preserving its functional form.
75	
76	\begin{figure}[t]
77	\centering
78	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig1_differential_response.pdf}
79	\caption{Differential depth response: Spearman correlations between individual features and generation depth. Central-tendency and diversity features (hapax ratio, TTR, entropy mean) show substantial monotonic drift; entropy std ($|\rho|{=}0.17$) occupies an intermediate position; shape features (entropy max, tail decay) remain near-stationary ($|\rho| < 0.10$).}
80	\label{fig:feature-drift}
81	\end{figure}
82	
83	Pooled class-balanced Spearman correlations with depth confirm the split (Figure~\ref{fig:feature-drift}):
84	drifting features show substantial monotonic trends (hapax ratio $\rho = -0.63$, TTR $\rho = -0.61$, entropy mean $\rho = -0.60$),
85	while shape features remain near-stationary (tail decay $|\rho| = 0.04$); entropy std occupies an intermediate position ($|\rho| = 0.17$).
86	
87	\paragraph{Distributional shift quantification.}
88	Two-sample Kolmogorov--Smirnov tests between gen-1 and gen-2+ distributions isolate the recursive depth effect (excluding the initial human$\rightarrow$LLM transition): drifting features exhibit large distributional shifts (entropy mean KS\,=\,0.537, TTR\,=\,0.517, hapax ratio\,=\,0.516; all $p \approx 0$), while shape features show order-of-magnitude smaller shifts (entropy max KS\,=\,0.043, tail decay\,=\,0.052).
89	All seven features reach statistical significance after Bonferroni correction, but shape-feature KS statistics are ${\sim}10\times$ smaller than drifting features, supporting the ``approximately stationary'' characterization.
90	Entropy std occupies an intermediate position (KS\,=\,0.285), consistent with its borderline rank correlation.
91	Levene tests for variance homogeneity reject equal variance for all seven features ($p < 10^{-10}$), indicating that recursive generation alters spread as well as location, though the magnitude of variance change is substantially smaller for shape features (coefficient of variation shift $<$3\% for entropy max vs.\ $>$15\% for hapax ratio).
92	
93	\paragraph{Feature complementarity.}
94	Under 10-fold stratified CV, the full 7-feature set (F1\,=\,0.773) significantly outperforms surface-only (F1\,=\,0.688) and reference-only (F1\,=\,0.713; McNemar $p \approx 0$; Table~\ref{tab:ablation}).
95	Reference-only features yield higher binary F1 (0.833 vs.\ 0.695 for the full ensemble) because surface lexical features introduce noise at the gen-1/gen-2+ boundary.
96	However, surface features are essential for 3-class classification, where they improve gen-0 discrimination by capturing vocabulary diversity patterns absent in reference-model entropy statistics (Full 3-class F1\,=\,0.773 vs.\ Reference-only 0.713).
97	Permutation importance: hapax ratio (0.35), entropy mean (0.23), TTR (0.18).
98	Leave-one-feature-out confirms 6 of 7 features contribute positively (Appendix~\ref{app:lofo}).
99	
100	\paragraph{Cross-family reference models.}
101	Alternative references (OLMo-2-1B, Qwen3-0.6B) achieve comparable LOGO F1 (spread 0.013; Table~\ref{tab:cross-ref}), with consistently negative entropy drift, confirming DDR is a property of recursive generation.
102	
103	\paragraph{Temperature robustness.}
104	The method operates reliably within $t \in [0.5, 0.9]$: F1 varies by at most 1\,pp from the default $t{=}0.7$ across this range (Appendix~\ref{app:sampling-sensitivity}).
105	At $t{=}1.0$, performance degrades by ${\sim}6$\,pp as the primary depth signal collapses: entropy mean's Spearman correlation with depth drops from $\rho = -0.60$ to $\rho = -0.002$, indicating that high-temperature sampling injects sufficient token-level randomness to mask the systematic location shift that DDR exploits.
106	
107	\input{table4}
108

110	\centering
111	\footnotesize
112	\begin{tabular}{lcc}
113	\toprule
114	\textbf{Feature set} & \textbf{F1} & \textbf{Top feature (imp.)} \\
115	\midrule
116	Full (7 features)     & 0.773 & hapax\_ratio (0.35) \\
117	Surface only (2)      & 0.688 & hapax\_ratio (0.41) \\
118	Reference only (5)    & 0.713 & entropy\_mean (0.27) \\
119	\bottomrule
120	\end{tabular}
121	\caption{Feature ablation (8-gen, 10-fold stratified CV, 3-class). Full $>$ surface (+8.5\,pp) and Full $>$ reference (+6.0\,pp) confirms complementarity.}
122	\label{tab:ablation}
123	\end{table}
124	
125	
126	\subsection{Comparison with Detection Baselines}
127	\label{sec:results-baselines}
128	
129	Can existing detection methods, designed for binary classification, also discriminate generation depth?
130	We repurpose four detection scores as ordinal depth predictors using the same XGBoost framework and identical LOGO splits (Table~\ref{tab:baselines}, Figure~\ref{fig:baseline-ci}).
131	
132	\begin{figure}[t]
133	\centering
134	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig3_baseline_comparison.png}
135	\caption{LOGO binary F1 with 95\% bootstrap CIs. Per-generator variance is higher for single detectors than for the ensemble.}
136	\label{fig:baseline-ci}
137	\end{figure}
138	
139	\begin{table}[t]
140	\centering
141	\footnotesize
142	\begin{tabular}{lcccc}
143	\toprule
144	\textbf{Method} & \textbf{Feat.} & \textbf{3-cl F1} & \textbf{Bin F1} & \textbf{ms/text} \\
145	\midrule
146	4-det.\ + surface     & 6 & \textbf{0.795} & \textbf{0.708} & 22.8 \\
147	4-detector ens.\      & 4 & 0.785 & 0.700 & 22.8 \\
148	7-feature (ours)      & 7 & 0.774 & 0.705 & \textbf{9.7} \\
149	Avg-LogProb           & 1 & 0.707 & 0.673 & --- \\
150	FastDetectGPT         & 1 & 0.660 & 0.569 & --- \\
151	LD-Score              & 1 & 0.630 & 0.675 & --- \\
152	Binoculars            & 1 & 0.411 & 0.539 & --- \\
153	\bottomrule
154	\end{tabular}
155	\caption{Depth classification (8-gen pooled LOGO). All methods use XGBoost with $n_\text{estimators}{=}200$, $\max_\text{depth}{=}6$. Binary: gen-1 vs.\ gen-2+. Inference cost: 7-feature requires one 410M reference model (905\,MB); 4-det ensembles require two models totaling 1.4B parameters (2{,}868\,MB).}
156	\label{tab:baselines}
157	\end{table}
158	
159	Our 7-feature ensemble significantly outperforms all individual detection baselines (Avg-LogProb, FastDetectGPT, Binoculars, LD-Score) by 6--11\,pp in 3-class F1.
160	The multi-detector ensembles (4-det, 4-det+surface) achieve moderately higher 3-class F1 (0.785--0.795 vs.\ 0.774), while binary performance is statistically indistinguishable ($\leq$0.3\,pp).
161	Critically, our approach requires only a single lightweight reference model (Pythia-410M, 410M parameters) with 2.4$\times$ faster inference and 3.2$\times$ lower GPU memory than the best multi-detector ensemble, which requires two models totaling 1.4B parameters.
162	
163	
164	\subsection{Downstream Utility}
165	\label{sec:results-downstream}
166	
167	If depth fingerprinting reliably identifies recursive text, can it improve data curation?
168	Fine-tuning OLMo-2-0425-1B on five data variants (3 seeds each) yields two conclusions.
169	At equal sample count ($n$\,=\,3{,}951), depth-aware selection is practically equivalent to random subsampling (ppl 2.387 vs.\ 2.367; TOST $\pm$2\% relative margin, $p = 1.1 \times 10^{-4}$).
170	However, data quantity dominates: the full dataset ($n$\,=\,12{,}299, ppl\,=\,2.335) yields significantly lower perplexity than any depth-filtered subset, and TOST rejects equivalence at the same margin (Appendix~\ref{app:downstream}).
171	Depth composition per se does not determine training utility at fixed sample count.
172

Cross-model chains avoid the gen-0 seed sharing issue present in single-generator LOGO.}\n\\label{tab:cross-model-perclass}\n\\end{table}\n\nLeave-one-family-out (LOFO) evaluation yields average binary F1\\,=\\,0.686 ($\\Delta$\\,=\\,$-$0.8\\,pp vs.\\ LOGO), confirming transfer across architectural families under the strictest unseen-generator test.\nPer-family LOGO performance reveals a scale effect: Pythia generators (1B, 2.8B) achieve the highest binary F1 (0.718--0.724), followed by Qwen (0.691--0.709) and OLMo (0.695), while larger generators Qwen3-8B (0.692) and Mistral-7B (0.633) show degradation.\nThis pattern is consistent with higher-capacity models generating text closer to the human baseline distribution, compressing the feature margins that DDR exploits.\nAppendix~\\ref{app:domain} further demonstrates that the signal generalizes across text domains, though with substantial variation (Wikipedia binary F1\\,=\\,0.789 vs.\\ Academic 0.628 at $t{=}0.7$; all domains $>$\\,0.73 at $t{=}1.0$).\n\n\\paragraph{Length confound control.}\nLength-matched subsampling yields binary F1\\,=\\,0.724 (vs.\\ 0.770 unmatched; $\\Delta$\\,=\\,$-$0.046); a random-subsample control at equal $N$ (F1\\,=\\,0.767) confirms the drop reflects deconfounding, not sample-size reduction.\nLength accounts for $\\approx$6\\% of discriminative performance (Appendix~\\ref{app:length}).\n\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\footnotesize\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{llcccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Model} & \\textbf{Family} & \\textbf{Params} & \\textbf{3-cl F1} & \\textbf{Bin F1} & \\textbf{95\\% CI} \\\\\n\\midrule\nPythia-2.8B     & Pythia  & 2.8B & 0.798 & \\textbf{0.724} & [.707, .742] \\\\\nPythia-1B       & Pythia  & 1.0B & \\textbf{0.806} & 0.718 & [.700, .735] \\\\\nQwen3-1.7B     & Qwen    & 1.7B & 0.772 & 0.709 & [.693, .726] \\\\\nLlama-3.2-3B   & Llama   & 3.2B & 0.775 & 0.697 & [.680, .713] \\\\\nOLMo-2-1B      & OLMo    & 1.5B & 0.781 & 0.695 & [.679, .711] \\\\\nQwen3-8B       & Qwen    & 8.2B & 0.714 & 0.692 & [.674, .708] \\\\\nQwen3-0.6B     & Qwen    & 0.6B & 0.780 & 0.691 & [.673, .709] \\\\\nMistral-7B     & Mistral & 7.0B & 0.682 & 0.633 & [.617, .648] \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{3}{l}{\\textbf{Average (8-gen LOGO)}} & \\textbf{0.762} & \\textbf{0.694} & [.688, .700] \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\caption{8-generator LOGO macro-F1 for the 7-feature ensemble (bootstrap 95\\% CIs, $n$\\,=\\,1000). Binary F1 (gen-1 vs.\\ gen-2+) is the primary metric. Note: 3-class F1 may carry upward bias from gen-0 seed sharing across generators; binary F1 is leak-free. Cross-model chains (Table~\\ref{tab:cross-ref}) use sample-level CV without this issue.}\n\\label{tab:logo-results}\n\\end{table}\n\nOrdinal regression methods (CORAL \\cite{coral_ordinal}, Frank-Hall \\cite{frank_hall_ordinal}) achieve comparable F1 with overlapping CIs, confirming that 3-class granularity provides insufficient resolution for ranking-aware losses.\n\n\\subsection{Differential Depth Response}\n\\label{sec:results-differential}\n\nFeatures exhibit \\emph{differential depth response} (DDR): central-tendency and diversity measures drift monotonically with depth, while shape measures remain near-stationary, consistent with recursive generation inducing a location shift in the NLL distribution while preserving its functional form.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig1_differential_response.pdf}\n\\caption{Differential depth response: Spearman correlations between individual features and generation depth. Central-tendency and diversity features (hapax ratio, TTR, entropy mean) show substantial monotonic drift; entropy std ($|\\rho|{=}0.17$) occupies an intermediate position; shape features (entropy max, tail decay) remain near-stationary ($|\\rho| < 0.10$).}\n\\label{fig:feature-drift}\n\\end{figure}\n\nPooled class-balanced Spearman correlations with depth confirm the split (Figure~\\ref{fig:feature-drift}):\ndrifting features show substantial monotonic trends (hapax ratio $\\rho = -0.63$, TTR $\\rho = -0.61$, entropy mean $\\rho = -0.60$),\nwhile shape features remain near-stationary (tail decay $|\\rho| = 0.04$); entropy std occupies an intermediate position ($|\\rho| = 0.17$).\n\n\\paragraph{Distributional shift quantification.}\nTwo-sample Kolmogorov--Smirnov tests between gen-1 and gen-2+ distributions isolate the recursive depth effect (excluding the initial human$\\rightarrow$LLM transition): drifting features exhibit large distributional shifts (entropy mean KS\\,=\\,0.537, TTR\\,=\\,0.517, hapax ratio\\,=\\,0.516; all $p \\approx 0$), while shape features show order-of-magnitude smaller shifts (entropy max KS\\,=\\,0.043, tail decay\\,=\\,0.052).\nAll seven features reach statistical significance after Bonferroni correction, but shape-feature KS statistics are ${\\sim}10\\times$ smaller than drifting features, supporting the ``approximately stationary'' characterization.\nEntropy std occupies an intermediate position (KS\\,=\\,0.285), consistent with its borderline rank correlation.\nLevene tests for variance homogeneity reject equal variance for all seven features ($p < 10^{-10}$), indicating that recursive generation alters spread as well as location, though the magnitude of variance change is substantially smaller for shape features (coefficient of variation shift $<$3\\% for entropy max vs.\\ $>$15\\% for hapax ratio).\n\n\\paragraph{Feature complementarity.}\nUnder 10-fold stratified CV, the full 7-feature set (F1\\,=\\,0.773) significantly outperforms surface-only (F1\\,=\\,0.688) and reference-only (F1\\,=\\,0.713; McNemar $p \\approx 0$; Table~\\ref{tab:ablation}).\nReference-only features yield higher binary F1 (0.833 vs.\\ 0.695 for the full ensemble) because surface lexical features introduce noise at the gen-1/gen-2+ boundary.\nHowever, surface features are essential for 3-class classification, where they improve gen-0 discrimination by capturing vocabulary diversity patterns absent in reference-model entropy statistics (Full 3-class F1\\,=\\,0.773 vs.\\ Reference-only 0.713).\nPermutation importance: hapax ratio (0.35), entropy mean (0.23), TTR (0.18).\nLeave-one-feature-out confirms 6 of 7 features contribute positively (Appendix~\\ref{app:lofo}).\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-family reference models.}\nAlternative references (OLMo-2-1B, Qwen3-0.6B) achieve comparable LOGO F1 (spread 0.013; Table~\\ref{tab:cross-ref}), with consistently negative entropy drift, confirming DDR is a property of recursive generation.\n\n\\paragraph{Temperature robustness.}\nThe method operates reliably within $t \\in [0.5, 0.9]$: F1 varies by at most 1\\,pp from the default $t{=}0.7$ across this range (Appendix~\\ref{app:sampling-sensitivity}).\nAt $t{=}1.0$, performance degrades by ${\\sim}6$\\,pp as the primary depth signal collapses: entropy mean's Spearman correlation with depth drops from $\\rho = -0.60$ to $\\rho = -0.002$, indicating that high-temperature sampling injects sufficient token-level randomness to mask the systematic location shift that DDR exploits.\n\n\\input{table4}\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\footnotesize\n\\begin{tabular}{lcc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Feature set} & \\textbf{F1} & \\textbf{Top feature (imp.)} \\\\\n\\midrule\nFull (7 features)     & 0.773 & hapax\\_ratio (0.35) \\\\\nSurface only (2)      & 0.688 & hapax\\_ratio (0.41) \\\\\nReference only (5)    & 0.713 & entropy\\_mean (0.27) \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Feature ablation (8-gen, 10-fold stratified CV, 3-class). Full $>$ surface (+8.5\\,pp) and Full $>$ reference (+6.0\\,pp) confirms complementarity.}\n\\label{tab:ablation}\n\\end{table}\n\n\n\\subsection{Comparison with Detection Baselines}\n\\label{sec:results-baselines}\n\nCan existing detection methods, designed for binary classification, also discriminate generation depth?\nWe repurpose four detection scores as ordinal depth predictors using the same XGBoost framework and identical LOGO splits (Table~\\ref{tab:baselines}, Figure~\\ref{fig:baseline-ci}).\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig3_baseline_comparison.png}\n\\caption{LOGO binary F1 with 95\\% bootstrap CIs. Per-generator variance is higher for single detectors than for the ensemble.}\n\\label{fig:baseline-ci}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\footnotesize\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Method} & \\textbf{Feat.} & \\textbf{3-cl F1} & \\textbf{Bin F1} & \\textbf{ms/text} \\\\\n\\midrule\n4-det.\\ + surface     & 6 & \\textbf{0.795} & \\textbf{0.708} & 22.8 \\\\\n4-detector ens.\\      & 4 & 0.785 & 0.700 & 22.8 \\\\\n7-feature (ours)      & 7 & 0.774 & 0.705 & \\textbf{9.7} \\\\\nAvg-LogProb           & 1 & 0.707 & 0.673 & --- \\\\\nFastDetectGPT         & 1 & 0.660 & 0.569 & --- \\\\\nLD-Score              & 1 & 0.630 & 0.675 & --- \\\\\nBinoculars            & 1 & 0.411 & 0.539 & --- \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Depth classification (8-gen pooled LOGO). All methods use XGBoost with $n_\\text{estimators}{=}200$, $\\max_\\text{depth}{=}6$. Binary: gen-1 vs.\\ gen-2+. Inference cost: 7-feature requires one 410M reference model (905\\,MB); 4-det ensembles require two models totaling 1.4B parameters (2{,}868\\,MB).}\n\\label{tab:baselines}\n\\end{table}\n\nOur 7-feature ensemble significantly outperforms all individual detection baselines (Avg-LogProb, FastDetectGPT, Binoculars, LD-Score) by 6--11\\,pp in 3-class F1.\nThe multi-detector ensembles (4-det, 4-det+surface) achieve moderately higher 3-class F1 (0.785--0.795 vs.\\ 0.774), while binary performance is statistically indistinguishable ($\\leq$0.3\\,pp).\nCritically, our approach requires only a single lightweight reference model (Pythia-410M, 410M parameters) with 2.4$\\times$ faster inference and 3.2$\\times$ lower GPU memory than the best multi-detector ensemble, which requires two models totaling 1.4B parameters.\n\n\n\\subsection{Downstream Utility}\n\\label{sec:results-downstream}\n\nIf depth fingerprinting reliably identifies recursive text, can it improve data curation?\nFine-tuning OLMo-2-0425-1B on five data variants (3 seeds each) yields two conclusions.\nAt equal sample count ($n$\\,=\\,3{,}951), depth-aware selection is practically equivalent to random subsampling (ppl 2.387 vs.\\ 2.367; TOST $\\pm$2\\% relative margin, $p = 1.1 \\times 10^{-4}$).\nHowever, data quantity dominates: the full dataset ($n$\\,=\\,12{,}299, ppl\\,=\\,2.335) yields significantly lower perplexity than any depth-filtered subset, and TOST rejects equivalence at the same margin (Appendix~\\ref{app:downstream}).\nDepth composition per se does not determine training utility at fixed sample count.\n",

1	\section*{Ethics Statement}
2	\label{sec:ethics}
3	
4	All texts analyzed in this work are LLM-generated from publicly available Wikipedia seeds; no personal or sensitive data is involved.
5	Our method estimates the generation depth of a text passage (how many recursive generation steps it has undergone), not the identity of its author.
6	Individual authorship attribution is outside both the scope and the capability of this approach; privacy implications are minimal.
7	We acknowledge that depth estimation tools could in principle be used to selectively suppress machine-generated text in contexts where such content is legitimate (e.g., accessibility tools, assistive writing).
8	We encourage deployment in data governance pipelines where provenance transparency, rather than content removal, is the objective.
9	
10	\paragraph{Licenses.}
11	All generator models are publicly available under permissive licenses (Apache 2.0 for Pythia, OLMo, and Mistral; Qwen License for Qwen3; Llama Community License for Llama 3.2).
12	Wikipedia seed passages are distributed under CC BY-SA 3.0.
13	
14	\paragraph{Compute budget.}
15	All experiments were conducted on 4$\times$ NVIDIA RTX 5090 GPUs (32\,GB each), totaling approximately 62 GPU-hours.
16	
17	\paragraph{Software.}
18	Experiments used Python 3.12.3, PyTorch 2.10.0 (CUDA 12.8), Transformers 4.57.6, XGBoost 3.2.0, scikit-learn 1.8.0, NumPy 2.2.6, pandas 3.0.2, and SciPy 1.17.1.

```text
1	\section{Conclusion}
2	\label{sec:conclusion}
3	
4	We introduced ordinal generation depth estimation and documented a differential depth response across eight base-model generators from five families: recursive generation induces a systematic location shift in the token-level NLL distribution while preserving distributional shape, and this structured drift is detectable with a lightweight 7-feature ensemble (3-class F1\,=\,0.809 on cross-model chains, 0.762 under LOGO).
5	Despite this detectable drift, depth-aware data curation is practically equivalent to random subsampling at equal sample count (TOST $\pm$2\%, $p < 0.001$) within chains of $\leq$4 generations as measured by perplexity, establishing that detectability and downstream harm are decoupled in this regime.
6	
7	Two boundaries define the scope of these results.
8	First, instruction tuning compresses the distributional variation that fingerprinting exploits; extending to aligned models requires disentangling format-induced and depth-induced drift.
9	Second, while the signal generalizes across generators up to 8B parameters, frontier-scale models (70B+) with higher fluency may further compress feature margins.
10	All code, data, and trained models will be released upon publication.
11	
```

</details>

      "content": "\\section{Conclusion}\n\\label{sec:conclusion}\n\nWe introduced ordinal generation depth estimation and documented a differential depth response across eight base-model generators from five families: recursive generation induces a systematic location shift in the token-level NLL distribution while preserving distributional shape, and this structured drift is detectable with a lightweight 7-feature ensemble (3-class F1\\,=\\,0.809 on cross-model chains, 0.762 under LOGO).\nDespite this detectable drift, depth-aware data curation is practically equivalent to random subsampling at equal sample count (TOST $\\pm$2\\%, $p < 0.001$) within chains of $\\leq$4 generations as measured by perplexity, establishing that detectability and downstream harm are decoupled in this regime.\n\nTwo boundaries define the scope of these results.\nFirst, instruction tuning compresses the distributional variation that fingerprinting exploits; extending to aligned models requires disentangling format-induced and depth-induced drift.\nSecond, while the signal generalizes across generators up to 8B parameters, frontier-scale models (70B+) with higher fluency may further compress feature margins.\nAll code, data, and trained models will be released upon publication.\n",
      "numLines": 11,
      "startLine": 1,
      "totalLines": 11
    }
  },
  "sourceToolAssistantUUID": "414f482f-2043-4967-8568-0b54c6f350b8",

```text
1	\section*{Limitations}
2	\label{sec:limitations}
3	
4	All eight generators are base (pretrained) models.
5	This is a deliberate design choice: base models isolate the generation mechanism from instruction-tuning effects, enabling controlled study of recursive distributional drift.
6	However, web text is predominantly produced by instruction-tuned and RLHF-aligned models.
7	Preliminary instruct experiments confirm that depth signal degrades substantially under instruction tuning: OLMo-2-1B-Instruct drops to LOGO F1\,=\,0.338 (vs.\ 0.763 base) and Qwen3-0.6B with chat template to 0.667 (vs.\ 0.816 under a 7-generator split).
8	Length-matched analysis yields near-zero correlations ($|\rho| < 0.02$) and binary classification near chance (F1\,$\approx$\,0.5) for Qwen models, leaving interpretation ambiguous.
9	Extending depth fingerprinting to instruct models requires addressing these confounds (length regulation, format constraints, safety-driven distribution shaping) that instruction tuning introduces.
10	
11	Our experiments include generators up to 8.2B parameters (Qwen3-8B).
12	Depth signal persists at this scale (Qwen3-8B 3-class F1\,=\,0.714), but cross-scale transfer incurs a moderate gap vs.\ $\leq$2.8B generators (F1\,=\,0.77--0.81).
13	Frontier models (70B+) remain untested; their higher fluency may further compress feature margins.
14	
15	Differential depth response strength is prompt-type dependent: continuation chains show strongest drift (within-type F1\,=\,0.781) compared to elaboration (0.730), rewriting (0.737), and paraphrastic chains (binary F1\,=\,0.671 vs.\ 0.749 for continuation).
16	DDR is most reliably detected in continuation-style generation; near-paraphrastic settings approach the method's detection floor.
17	Cross-prompt-type transfer (LOPTO F1\,=\,0.753) confirms shared signal, yet additional prompt types (e.g., summarization, instruction following) and cross-generator $\times$ cross-prompt interactions remain untested.
18	
19	Our primary evaluation uses single-generator chains; cross-model chains (\S\ref{sec:results-classification}) confirm depth signal under generator heterogeneity (3-class F1\,=\,0.809, binary F1\,=\,0.764), though the eight generators represent a controlled laboratory setting rather than the full diversity of real-world contamination.
20	Simulated human editing (\S\ref{sec:discussion}) suggests graceful degradation, but the gap between simulated token replacement and real-world post-processing remains untested.
21	
22	The reference model is fixed to Pythia-410M throughout, although cross-family reference experiments (\S\ref{sec:results-differential}) confirm the phenomenon is not reference-specific (spread 0.013 across three references).
23	All seed texts in the main evaluation are English Wikipedia excerpts.
24	Cross-domain evaluation (Appendix~\ref{app:domain}) shows that depth signal persists across News, Academic, and Code domains (binary F1 $>$ 0.73 under LOGO at $t{=}1.0$), though a domain $\times$ temperature interaction exists: certain domain/generator combinations at lower temperature approach chance level (e.g., Academic domain binary F1\,=\,0.628 at $t{=}0.7$), suggesting that reference model calibration is critical for cross-domain deployment.
25	The reference model dependency (Pythia-410M, English-centric pretraining) limits non-English applicability and adds inference overhead relative to surface-feature-only approaches.
26	Generalization to other languages remains untested.
27	
28	Our default analysis uses fixed decoding parameters (temperature 0.7, nucleus $p{=}0.9$). A sampling ablation (Appendix~\ref{app:sampling-sensitivity}) shows robustness to moderate variation ($t \in [0.5, 0.7]$, ${\leq}0.3$\,pp) but substantial degradation at $t{=}1.0$ ($-6$\,pp), indicating that depth fingerprinting assumes standard decoding regimes.
29	
30	We use 3-class granularity (gen-0\,/\,gen-1\,/\,gen-2+). A 5-class evaluation (Appendix~\ref{app:finer-depth}) yields LOGO F1\,$\approx$\,0.50, with depths 2--4 mutually indistinguishable, validating the 3-class design as the practical resolution limit.
31	
```

The LOFO section needs to focus on the key finding—that transfer works across architectures but with a scale effect where smaller models perform better—and I can move the domain generalization details to the appendix reference rather than spelling them out here. The full feature set substantially outperforms both surface-only and reference-only baselines in 10-fold cross-validation, though reference features alone achieve surprisingly high binary F1 because surface lexical cues introduce noise at the generation boundary. Surface features become critical for 3-class discrimination, where they capture vocabulary diversity patterns that reference-model entropy misses. Permutation importance analysis shows hapax ratio as the dominant contributor. Looking at the permutation importance scores—hapax ratio dominates at 0.35, followed by entropy mean and type-token ratio—and the leave-one-feature-out analysis confirms that six of the seven features provide meaningful contributions to the model.

We adapt four existing detection baselines as ordinal depth predictors within the same XGBoost framework and evaluation setup.

Our 7-feature ensemble substantially outperforms individual detection baselines by 6–11 percentage points on 3-class F1, while multi-detector combinations achieve only marginal improvements. The key advantage is computational efficiency—we need just a single lightweight reference model that's 2.4× faster than alternatives. The fixed softmax architecture and sampling hyperparameters keep the generation mechanism consistent across depths, so recursive generation shifts toward high-probability regions without fundamentally changing how the model generates—this explains the approximate stationarity we see with standard decoding, which the sampling ablation confirms (minimal degradation at temperatures 0.5-0.7, but notable drops at 1.0). Entropy standard deviation sits between the drifting features and the nearly stationary tail decay, suggesting it partially captures location shifts rather than being fully stable. The gen-1/gen-2+ boundary shows a clear asymmetry: most errors are false negatives where gen-2+ gets misclassified as gen-1, and this pattern intensifies at shallow depths because the first recursive step produces minimal feature space shift, causing depth-2 text to cluster near gen-1 regions. Larger models worsen this overlap since they generate text statistically closer to human input, making the distinction harder.

Now looking at model collapse—this is fundamentally different from DDR since model collapse involves retraining on outputs and causes permanent distributional degradation, whereas DDR happens during inference with fixed parameters and can be reversed by changing the seed text.

The key distinction is in the distributional signatures: model collapse predicts progressive tail shrinkage as minority modes disappear, but DDR maintains the same distributional shape while just shifting the location. This suggests DDR is more about compression within the model's existing capacity rather than erosion of that capacity—possibly a pre-collapse regime where narrowing is detectable but still reversible.

Topic drift shows weak correlation with depth features, and embedding similarity between generations correlates with entropy drift, confirming that topic drift mediates the depth signal rather than confounding it. The richer signal exists in the generative structure itself—LLM-simulated regeneration with 50% edit distance produces similar degradation to random word replacement, confirming that depth information isn't captured by surface-level token statistics alone.

Now I'm thinking through space savings across the paper. Removing the cross-model comparison table frees up roughly 0.4 columns, and compressing the LOFO and scale discussion saves another few lines. I'm also considering whether moving the baseline confidence interval plot to the appendix would be worth the additional 0.4 columns of space.

Looking at my total savings so far—about 1 column across results and discussion sections—I realize I need to cut roughly twice that amount. <details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01MytHdEMiCBcqKtLXE5NScK`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Method}
2	\label{sec:method}
3	
4	\begin{figure*}[t]
5	\centering
6	\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{fig2_framework.pdf}
7	\caption{Overview of the depth fingerprinting framework. Given a text, reference-model features (entropy statistics under Pythia-410M) and surface features (lexical diversity) are extracted and fed to an XGBoost classifier trained under Leave-One-Generator-Out cross-validation. The feature set combines drifting indicators (entropy mean, TTR, hapax ratio) that capture the monotonic location shift with stationary indicators (entropy max, tail decay) that provide a stable baseline, exploiting their differential depth response for ordinal classification.}
8	\label{fig:framework}
9	\end{figure*}
10	
11	\subsection{Task Formulation}
12	\label{sec:task}
13	
14	Given a text $x$, the goal is to predict its \emph{generation depth} $d \in \{0, 1, 2{+}\}$, where $d{=}0$ denotes human-written text, $d{=}1$ denotes first-generation LLM output conditioned on human text, and $d{=}2{+}$ denotes text produced by recursively conditioning an LLM on its own prior output at least twice.
15	Formally, let $\mathcal{C}_0$ be a reference corpus of human-written text.
16	A generation chain of depth $k$ is defined recursively: $x^{(0)} \sim \mathcal{C}_0$, and $x^{(k)} = G(x^{(k-1)})$ for $k \geq 1$, where $G$ denotes stochastic LLM generation conditioned on its input.
17	We frame depth estimation as an ordinal classification task over three classes rather than binary real-vs-synthetic detection, enabling finer-grained provenance characterization.
18	Depths beyond 2 are merged into a single $2{+}$ class, consistent with monotonic feature convergence that compresses inter-class margins beyond depth 2 (\S\ref{sec:results-differential}).
19	
20	\subsection{Generation Chain Construction}
21	\label{sec:generation}
22	
23	We construct generation chains using eight generators spanning five model families: \textbf{Pythia} (2.8B, 1B) \cite{pythia}, \textbf{OLMo} (OLMo-2-1B) \cite{olmo}, \textbf{Qwen} (Qwen3-1.7B, Qwen3-0.6B, Qwen3-8B-Base) \cite{qwen3}, \textbf{Llama} (Llama-3.2-3B) \cite{llama3}, and \textbf{Mistral} (Mistral-7B-v0.3) \cite{mistral7b}.
24	This selection covers parameter scales from 0.6B to 8B and diverse pretraining corpora, enabling evaluation of cross-generator and cross-scale generalization.
25	
26	For each generator, we sample approximately 1{,}000 seed passages from English Wikipedia as $\mathcal{C}_0$.
27	The same pool of seed passages serves as gen-0 for all generators.
28	We use shared seeds across generators to ensure that cross-generator comparisons control for input content, isolating the effect of the generation model from source text variation.
29	In LOGO evaluation, gen-0 samples from the held-out generator's chains also appear (with the same text) in the training generators' chains, creating a potential train--test overlap for the gen-0 class.
30	We address this with a supplementary binary evaluation excluding gen-0 entirely (\S\ref{sec:results-baselines}).
31	Each seed is iteratively fed through the generator to produce a chain of depth 4 (i.e., gen-0 through gen-4), using the prompt ``\texttt{Continue the following text: \{text\}}''.
32	All generation uses temperature $\tau{=}0.7$, nucleus sampling with $p{=}0.9$, and maximum length of 256 new tokens.
33	The theoretical yield is $8 \times {\sim}1{,}000 \times 5 \approx 40{,}000$ texts; after removing generation failures, exact duplicates, and passages shorter than 50 tokens, 37{,}058 texts remain (Appendix~\ref{app:dataset-stats}).
34	For classification, depths 2--4 are collapsed into the $2{+}$ class.
35	
36	\subsection{Feature Extraction}
37	\label{sec:features}
38	
39	The seven features are motivated by two complementary views of distributional drift.
40	Reference-model features capture how the text's token-level surprise distribution shifts relative to a fixed model; surface features capture how lexical diversity contracts under recursive generation.
41	Together, they span both the generative mechanism (what the model produces) and the surface manifestation (how the vocabulary changes); Figure~\ref{fig:framework} provides an overview.
42	This two-pronged design is not post-hoc: it follows directly from the hypothesis that recursive generation induces a location shift in the NLL distribution (\S\ref{sec:results-differential}).
43	
44	We extract seven distributional features per text, organized into two groups based on what they measure rather than how they are computed.
45	
46	\paragraph{Reference-model features.}
47	A held-out reference model (Pythia-410M, chosen to be smaller than all generators) computes per-token negative log-likelihood (NLL) for each text.
48	Although Pythia-410M shares the GPT-NeoX architecture with two of the eight generators, cross-family reference model experiments (\S\ref{sec:results-differential}) confirm that depth features are not reference-specific (LOGO F1 varies by $\leq$0.013 across Pythia, OLMo, and Qwen references).
49	From the resulting NLL sequence, we extract five statistics:
50	\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=*]
51	    \item \textbf{entropy\_mean}\footnote{We use ``entropy'' as shorthand for token-level negative log-likelihood under the reference model, following convention in prior detection work. These are point-wise NLL values, not Shannon entropy of the full distribution.}: mean token-level NLL, capturing the average predictability of the text under the reference model;
52	    \item \textbf{entropy\_std}: standard deviation of token-level NLL, measuring the spread of per-token surprise;
53	    \item \textbf{entropy\_max}: maximum token-level NLL, capturing the most surprising token;
54	    \item \textbf{entropy\_min}: minimum token-level NLL, capturing the most predictable token. As generation depth increases, the floor of token-level surprise drops, reflecting the model's increasing confidence on its own outputs.
55	    Although entropy\_min shows near-zero marginal impact in leave-one-feature-out analysis ($\Delta$F1\,=\,+0.01\,pp), we retain it for completeness of the NLL summary statistics (mean, std, max, min) and because removing it does not improve performance;
56	    \item \textbf{tail\_decay}: slope of the vocabulary-level averaged log-probability distribution in the mid-rank region (ranks 100--1000).
57	    For each text, we compute the mean log-probability of each vocabulary token across all positions under the reference model, sort tokens in descending order of mean log-probability, and fit a linear regression to ranks 100--1000.
58	    The slope captures how steeply the reference model's probability mass decays beyond high-probability tokens, characterizing the shape of the distribution tail.
59	\end{itemize}
60	
61	\paragraph{Surface features.}
62	Two features capture lexical diversity without requiring a reference model:
63	\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=*]
64	    \item \textbf{TTR}: type-token ratio (number of unique word types divided by total tokens);
65	    \item \textbf{hapax\_ratio}: proportion of words appearing exactly once (\emph{hapax legomena}).
66	\end{itemize}
67	
68	\paragraph{Feature complementarity.}
69	The grouping reflects an empirical pattern we term \emph{differential depth response}: central-tendency and diversity features (entropy\_mean, TTR, hapax\_ratio) show substantial monotonic drift with generation depth ($|\rho| = 0.60$--$0.63$); entropy\_min and entropy\_std show moderate drift ($|\rho| = 0.17$--$0.41$); and shape features (entropy\_max, tail\_decay) remain near-stationary ($|\rho| < 0.10$).
70	This differential behavior, consistent with recursive generation shifting the location of the NLL distribution while preserving distributional shape, motivates combining both feature types.
71	As shown in \S\ref{sec:results}, the full 7-feature set significantly outperforms either group alone.
72	
73	\subsection{Evaluation Protocol}
74	\label{sec:evaluation}
75	
76	\paragraph{Cross-validation.}
77	To evaluate generalization to unseen generators, we use \textbf{Leave-One-Generator-Out (LOGO)} cross-validation: in each fold, one of the eight generators is held out for testing and the remaining seven provide training data.
78	We additionally report \textbf{Leave-One-Family-Out (LOFO)} results, where an entire model family (e.g., all Qwen models) is held out, testing generalization across architectural lineages.
79	
80	\paragraph{Classifier.}
81	We use XGBoost \cite{xgboost} as the classifier throughout.
82	Gradient-boosted trees handle heterogeneous feature scales and nonlinear interactions without extensive hyperparameter tuning, and their feature importance scores support interpretability.
83	All methods in Table~\ref{tab:baselines} use identical XGBoost hyperparameters ($n\_\text{estimators}{=}200$, $\max\_\text{depth}{=}6$, learning rate $0.1$) for controlled comparison.
84	LOGO F1 is stable across random seeds (std\,$<$\,0.001 over 5 seeds), confirming that results are not sensitive to classifier initialization.
85	Alternative classifiers (random forest, logistic regression) yield comparable performance (Appendix~\ref{app:classifier}), confirming that discriminative power resides in the features, not the classifier.
86	We evaluated two ordinal-specific methods (CORAL \cite{coral_ordinal}, Frank-Hall ordinal ensemble \cite{frank_hall_ordinal}); neither significantly outperforms multiclass XGBoost (CIs overlapping; \S\ref{sec:results}), confirming that with only three ordinal levels, ordinal structure provides minimal additional benefit.
87	
88	\paragraph{Metrics and statistical testing.}
89	The primary metric is \textbf{macro-averaged F1} over the three depth classes, which weights minority classes (gen-1, gen-2+) equally against the typically easier gen-0 class.
90	We report per-class F1 scores and 95\% confidence intervals via \emph{percentile bootstrap} ($n{=}1{,}000$), resampling at the document level within each held-out fold.
91	For pairwise model comparisons (e.g., full features vs.\ ablated subsets), we use the \textbf{McNemar test} on disagreement tables.
92	For comparisons against detection baselines, we use \textbf{paired bootstrap} significance tests.
93	All reported $p$-values are two-sided.
94	We report four pairwise comparisons (ensemble vs.\ each individual baseline) in \S\ref{sec:results-baselines}; applying Bonferroni correction ($\alpha_{\text{adj}} = 0.05/4 = 0.0125$), all remain significant at $p < 0.001$.
95	Feature--depth correlations (\S\ref{sec:results-differential}) are computed on class-balanced subsamples (equal samples per depth class) to control for depth-class imbalance in the generation pipeline.
96	
97	\paragraph{Baselines.}
98	We compare the 7-feature ensemble against four single-score detection methods repurposed as ordinal depth predictors: Avg-LogProb, FastDetectGPT \cite{fast_detectgpt}, Binoculars \cite{binoculars}, and LD-Score \cite{ld_score}.
99	Each baseline score is used as a univariate feature in the same XGBoost framework under LOGO cross-validation, ensuring a controlled comparison.
100	
101	\paragraph{Length confound control.}
102	Since TTR and hapax\_ratio are mechanically length-dependent, we verify that depth signal is not an artifact of systematic length variation.
103	We conduct three complementary analyses: (1) length--depth correlation to verify absence of systematic length variation, (2) length--feature correlation to identify features with moderate length dependence, and (3) classification on length-matched subsets that equalize length distributions across depth classes (\S\ref{sec:results-classification}).
104	
```
