```text
1	\section{Introduction}
2	\label{sec:introduction}
3	
4	Web-crawled pretraining corpora \cite{dolma,fineweb} increasingly contain LLM-generated text (estimates suggest that 7--17\% of recent scientific papers and peer reviews contain substantial LLM-modified content \cite{mapping_llm_science,monitoring_ai_review}), and this proportion grows as generated text re-enters the web and seeds subsequent generation.
5	The result is recursive generation chains whose outputs progressively diverge from human-written text.
6	Understanding how distributional features evolve across generation depths is a prerequisite for characterizing this recursive contamination, yet existing methods \cite{fast_detectgpt,binoculars,ld_score,detectgpt} provide only binary real-vs-synthetic decisions, conflating qualitatively different material: a first-generation continuation retains much of the source distribution's lexical properties, while deeper recursive text exhibits measurably reduced vocabulary diversity and compressed perplexity.
7	
8	We formulate \emph{ordinal depth estimation}, classifying text by generation depth (gen-0\,/\,gen-1\,/\,gen-2+) rather than a binary label, and refer to the overall framework as \emph{depth fingerprinting}.
9	No existing method addresses this task in non-paraphrastic continuation chains.
10	
11	The key finding is a \emph{differential depth response} (DDR): when we measure how eight base-model generators from five families (Pythia, OLMo, Qwen, Llama, Mistral; 0.6B--8B parameters) transform text across recursive depths, central-tendency and diversity features (reference-model mean entropy, type-token ratio, hapax ratio) drift monotonically, while shape features (entropy max, tail decay) remain near-stationary.
12	This pattern is consistent with recursive generation inducing a location shift in the token-level NLL distribution while preserving its shape: the softmax architecture and fixed sampling parameters constrain distributional shape under standard decoding regimes, while each successive generation compresses the location toward the model's preferred output distribution.
13	We restrict to base (pretrained) models as a deliberate design choice, since instruction-tuning introduces confounds (length regulation, format constraints, safety-driven shaping) that obscure this intrinsic mechanism; preliminary experiments confirm substantially degraded depth signal under instruction tuning (\S\ref{sec:discussion}).
14	
15	The combination of drifting and stationary features yields a 7-feature ensemble that achieves 3-class macro F1\,=\,0.809 on heterogeneous cross-model chains and F1\,=\,0.762 under leave-one-generator-out evaluation (binary F1\,=\,0.764 on cross-model chains), outperforming all individual detection baselines by 6--11\,pp while requiring only a single 410M-parameter reference model.
16	The signal is prompt-type dependent: continuation chains show stronger drift than elaboration or rewriting (\S\ref{sec:discussion}).
17	
18	Our contributions are:
19	\begin{enumerate}[nosep,leftmargin=*]
20	    \item \textbf{Task formulation.} We define ordinal generation depth estimation as a task beyond binary detection, providing a framework for characterizing how distributional properties evolve across recursive generation.
21	    \item \textbf{Empirical characterization.} We document differential depth response across eight generators from five families and show that a 7-feature ensemble exploiting this structure achieves competitive depth classification while requiring only a single small reference model; the interpretable feature design enables the DDR characterization in \S\ref{sec:results-differential}.
22	    \item \textbf{Detectable $\neq$ harmful.} Despite DDR producing measurable distributional drift, a TOST equivalence test confirms that depth-stratified text yields training utility equivalent to random subsampling ($\pm$2\% margin, $p < 0.001$) within chains of $\leq$4 generations as measured by perplexity (\S\ref{sec:results-downstream}), decoupling detectability from downstream harm.
23	\end{enumerate}
24	
```

Cross-model chains avoid the gen-0 seed sharing issue present in single-generator LOGO.}\n\\label{tab:cross-model-perclass}\n\\end{table}\n\nLeave-one-family-out (LOFO) evaluation yields average binary F1\\,=\\,0.686 ($\\Delta$\\,=\\,$-$0.8\\,pp vs.\\ LOGO), confirming transfer across architectural families under the strictest unseen-generator test.\nPer-family LOGO performance reveals a scale effect: Pythia generators (1B, 2.8B) achieve the highest binary F1 (0.718--0.724), followed by Qwen (0.691--0.709) and OLMo (0.695), while larger generators Qwen3-8B (0.692) and Mistral-7B (0.633) show degradation.\nThis pattern is consistent with higher-capacity models generating text closer to the human baseline distribution, compressing the feature margins that DDR exploits.\nAppendix~\\ref{app:domain} further demonstrates that the signal generalizes across text domains, though with substantial variation (Wikipedia binary F1\\,=\\,0.789 vs.\\ Academic 0.628 at $t{=}0.7$; all domains $>$\\,0.73 at $t{=}1.0$).",

  "old_string": "\\paragraph{Feature complementarity.}\nUnder 10-fold stratified CV, the full 7-feature set (F1\\,=\\,0.773) significantly outperforms surface-only (F1\\,=\\,0.688) and reference-only (F1\\,=\\,0.713; McNemar $p \\approx 0$; Table~\\ref{tab:ablation}).\nReference-only features yield higher binary F1 (0.833 vs.\\ 0.695 for the full ensemble) because surface lexical features introduce noise at the gen-1/gen-2+ boundary.\nHowever, surface features are essential for 3-class classification, where they improve gen-0 discrimination by capturing vocabulary diversity patterns absent in reference-model entropy statistics (Full 3-class F1\\,=\\,0.773 vs.\\ Reference-only 0.713).\nPermutation importance: hapax ratio (0.35), entropy mean (0.23), TTR (0.18).\nLeave-one-feature-out confirms 6 of 7 features contribute positively (Appendix~\\ref{app:lofo}).",
  "new_string": "\\paragraph{Feature complementarity.}\nUnder 10-fold stratified CV, the full 7-feature set (F1\\,=\\,0.773) outperforms surface-only (F1\\,=\\,0.688) and reference-only (F1\\,=\\,0.713; McNemar $p \\approx 0$; Table~\\ref{tab:ablation}).\nReference-only yields higher binary F1 (0.833 vs.\\ 0.695) since surface features introduce noise at the gen-1/gen-2+ boundary, but surface features are essential for 3-class gen-0 discrimination (Full 0.773 vs.\\ Reference-only 0.713).\nPermutation importance: hapax ratio (0.35), entropy mean (0.23), TTR (0.18); leave-one-feature-out confirms 6 of 7 features contribute positively (Appendix~\\ref{app:lofo})."
}

{
  "replace_all": false,
  "file_path": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_3/projects/gen-depth-fingerprint/artifacts/_project/paper/results.tex",
  "old_string": "Our 7-feature ensemble significantly outperforms all individual detection baselines (Avg-LogProb, FastDetectGPT, Binoculars, LD-Score) by 6--11\\,pp in 3-class F1.\nThe multi-detector ensembles (4-det, 4-det+surface) achieve moderately higher 3-class F1 (0.785--0.795 vs.\\ 0.774), while binary performance is statistically indistinguishable ($\\leq$0.3\\,pp).\nCritically, our approach requires only a single lightweight reference model (Pythia-410M, 410M parameters) with 2.4$\\times$ faster inference and 3.2$\\times$ lower GPU memory than the best multi-detector ensemble, which requires two models totaling 1.4B parameters.",
  "new_string": "Our 7-feature ensemble outperforms all individual baselines by 6--11\\,pp in 3-class F1.\nMulti-detector ensembles achieve moderately higher 3-class F1 (0.785--0.795 vs.\\ 0.774) with indistinguishable binary performance ($\\leq$0.3\\,pp), but require 2.4$\\times$ longer inference and 3.2$\\times$ more GPU memory (two models, 1.4B parameters vs.\\ our single 410M reference)."
}

  "old_string": "\\paragraph{Why does shape remain approximately stationary?}\nThe softmax architecture and sampling hyperparameters that determine distributional shape are fixed across depths; recursive generation shifts context toward high-probability regions but does not alter the generation mechanism itself.\nThis predicts approximate stationarity under standard decoding, confirmed by sampling ablation ($t \\in [0.5, 0.7]$: ${\\leq}0.3$\\,pp degradation; $t = 1.0$: $-6$\\,pp; Appendix~\\ref{app:sampling-sensitivity}), and implies the phenomenon reflects a tendency of standard decoding rather than an architectural guarantee.\nEntropy std ($|\\rho| = 0.17$) is a borderline case between the drifting central-tendency features ($|\\rho| > 0.60$) and the near-stationary tail decay ($|\\rho| = 0.04$), consistent with a feature that partially captures location-dependent variance.",

    "oldString": "\\paragraph{Why does shape remain approximately stationary?}\nThe softmax architecture and sampling hyperparameters that determine distributional shape are fixed across depths; recursive generation shifts context toward high-probability regions but does not alter the generation mechanism itself.\nThis predicts approximate stationarity under standard decoding, confirmed by sampling ablation ($t \\in [0.5, 0.7]$: ${\\leq}0.3$\\,pp degradation; $t = 1.0$: $-6$\\,pp; Appendix~\\ref{app:sampling-sensitivity}), and implies the phenomenon reflects a tendency of standard decoding rather than an architectural guarantee.\nEntropy std ($|\\rho| = 0.17$) is a borderline case between the drifting central-tendency features ($|\\rho| > 0.60$) and the near-stationary tail decay ($|\\rho| = 0.04$), consistent with a feature that partially captures location-dependent variance.",
    "newString": "\\paragraph{Why does shape remain approximately stationary?}\nThe softmax architecture and fixed sampling parameters determine distributional shape; recursive generation shifts context toward high-probability regions without altering the generation mechanism.\nSampling ablation confirms this ($t \\in [0.5, 0.7]$: ${\\leq}0.3$\\,pp degradation; $t = 1.0$: $-6$\\,pp; Appendix~\\ref{app:sampling-sensitivity}), indicating a tendency of standard decoding rather than an architectural guarantee.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\paragraph{Why does shape remain approximately stationary?}\nThe softmax architecture and sampling hyperparameters that determine distributional shape are fixed across depths; recursive generation shifts context toward high-probability regions but does not alter the generation mechanism itself.\nThis predicts approximate stationarity under standard decoding, confirmed by sampling ablation ($t \\in [0.5, 0.7]$: ${\\leq}0.3$\\,pp degradation; $t = 1.0$: $-6$\\,pp; Appendix~\\ref{app:sampling-sensitivity}), and implies the phenomenon reflects a tendency of standard decoding rather than an architectural guarantee.\nEntropy std ($|\\rho| = 0.17$) is a borderline case between the drifting central-tendency features ($|\\rho| > 0.60$) and the near-stationary tail decay ($|\\rho| = 0.04$), consistent with a feature that partially captures location-dependent variance.\n\n\\paragraph{Prompt-type dependence.}\nSemantic-preservation constraints attenuate drift (elaboration F1\\,=\\,0.730 vs.\\ continuation 0.781; Appendix~\\ref{app:prompt-type}), consistent with \\citet{broken_telephone}: the more a task anchors the output distribution to the input, the less room recursive drift has to accumulate.\nCross-type transfer remains viable (F1\\,=\\,0.753).\n\n\\paragraph{Practical applicability.}\nDocument-level gen-1 per-class F1\\,=\\,0.63 is insufficient for automated filtering but sufficient for corpus-level flagging, where error scales as $O(1/\\sqrt{n})$.\nMisclassifications concentrate in shallow gen-2+ samples where drift has not yet accumulated (Appendix~\\ref{app:error-analysis}), consistent with DDR predictions.\n\n\\paragraph{The gen-1\\,/\\,gen-2+ boundary.}\nThe primary classification difficulty is distinguishing gen-1 from gen-2+: 86\\% of binary errors are false negatives (gen-2+ predicted as gen-1), with depth-2 error rate at 48\\% compared to 20\\% for depth-4.\nThis asymmetry follows directly from DDR: the first recursive step produces the smallest location shift, so depth-2 text occupies a region of feature space that overlaps heavily with gen-1.\nLarger generators exacerbate the overlap (Mistral-7B error rate 45\\% vs.\\ Pythia-1B 18\\%), consistent with higher-capacity models producing text that more closely matches the statistical properties of human-sourced input.\n\n\\paragraph{Relation to model collapse.}\nModel collapse \\cite{curse_of_recursion,strong_model_collapse} describes degradation when models are \\emph{retrained} on their own outputs, progressively losing distributional coverage through parameter updates.\nDDR operates in a fundamentally different regime: inference-chain drift occurs with fixed parameters, and is fully reversible by changing the seed text.\nThe two phenomena also diverge in their distributional signatures: model collapse predicts progressive tail shrinkage as minority modes are lost from the training distribution \\cite{curse_of_recursion}, whereas DDR preserves distributional shape (entropy max, tail decay remain stationary) while shifting only the location.\nThis suggests that inference-chain drift reflects compression \\emph{within} the model's existing capacity rather than \\emph{erosion} of that capacity, and may represent a pre-collapse regime where distributional narrowing is detectable but not yet irreversible.\nTopic drift shows weak correlation with depth features ($|\\rho| < 0.29$; embedding similarity between successive generations correlates with entropy drift at $\\rho = -0.286$), confirming it mediates rather than confounds depth signal.\n\n\\paragraph{Granularity and robustness.}\nThree classes represent the practical resolution ceiling: 5-class LOGO F1 drops to $\\approx$0.50, with depths 2--4 mutually indistinguishable (Appendix~\\ref{app:finer-depth}).\nThat said, QLoRA fine-tuning recovers 3-class F1\\,=\\,0.905 (Appendix~\\ref{app:qlora}), confirming that richer signal exists beyond the seven summary statistics, though at the cost of interpretability and the DDR decomposition that is a core contribution of this work.\nUnder text perturbation, LLM-simulated sentence regeneration (50\\% edit distance) causes comparable degradation to 5\\% random word replacement (Appendix~\\ref{app:human-edit}), confirming that depth signal resides in generative structure rather than surface token statistics.\n\n\\paragraph{Instruction tuning.}\nInstruction-tuned models show substantially weaker fingerprints (OLMo-Instruct LOGO F1\\,=\\,0.338 vs.\\ 0.763 base).\nLength regulation and format constraints imposed by alignment compress the distributional variation that depth fingerprinting exploits, justifying our restriction to base models as the appropriate setting for isolating the phenomenon.\n",

occupies a region of feature space that overlaps heavily with gen-1.\nLarger generators exacerbate the overlap (Mistral-7B error rate 45\\% vs.\\ Pythia-1B 18\\%), consistent with higher-capacity models producing text that more closely matches the statistical properties of human-sourced input.\n\n\\paragraph{Relation to model collapse.}\nModel collapse \\cite{curse_of_recursion,strong_model_collapse} describes degradation when models are \\emph{retrained} on their own outputs, progressively losing distributional coverage through parameter updates.\nDDR operates in a fundamentally different regime: inference-chain drift occurs with fixed parameters, and is fully reversible by changing the seed text.\nThe two phenomena also diverge in their distributional signatures: model collapse predicts progressive tail shrinkage as minority modes are lost from the training distribution \\cite{curse_of_recursion}, whereas DDR preserves distributional shape (entropy max, tail decay remain stationary) while shifting only the location.\nThis suggests that inference-chain drift reflects compression \\emph{within} the model's existing capacity rather than \\emph{erosion} of that capacity, and may represent a pre-collapse regime where distributional narrowing is detectable but not yet irreversible.\nTopic drift shows weak correlation with depth features ($|\\rho| < 0.29$; embedding similarity between successive generations correlates with entropy drift at $\\rho = -0.286$), confirming it mediates rather than confounds depth signal.\n\n\\paragraph{Granularity and robustness.}\nThree classes represent the practical resolution ceiling: 5-class LOGO F1 drops to $\\approx$0.50, with depths 2--4 mutually indistinguishable (Appendix~\\ref{app:finer-depth}).\nThat said, QLoRA fine-tuning recovers 3-class F1\\,=\\,0.905 (Appendix~\\ref{app:qlora}), confirming that richer signal exists beyond the seven summary statistics, though at the cost of interpretability and the DDR decomposition that is a core contribution of this work.\nUnder text perturbation, LLM-simulated sentence regeneration (50\\% edit distance) causes comparable degradation to 5\\% random word replacement (Appendix~\\ref{app:human-edit}), confirming that depth signal resides in generative structure rather than surface token statistics.\n\n\\paragraph{Instruction tuning.}\nInstruction-tuned models show substantially weaker fingerprints (OLMo-Instruct LOGO F1\\,=\\,0.338 vs.\\ 0.763 base).\nLength regulation and format constraints imposed by alignment compress the distributional variation that depth fingerprinting exploits, justifying our restriction to base models as the appropriate setting for isolating the phenomenon.\n",

  "old_string": "\\paragraph{Metrics and statistical testing.}\nThe primary metric is \\textbf{macro-averaged F1} over the three depth classes, which weights minority classes (gen-1, gen-2+) equally against the typically easier gen-0 class.\nWe report per-class F1 scores and 95\\% confidence intervals via \\emph{percentile bootstrap} ($n{=}1{,}000$), resampling at the document level within each held-out fold.\nFor pairwise model comparisons (e.g., full features vs.\\ ablated subsets), we use the \\textbf{McNemar test} on disagreement tables.\nFor comparisons against detection baselines, we use \\textbf{paired bootstrap} significance tests.\nAll reported $p$-values are two-sided.\nWe report four pairwise comparisons (ensemble vs.\\ each individual baseline) in \\S\\ref{sec:results-baselines}; applying Bonferroni correction ($\\alpha_{\\text{adj}} = 0.05/4 = 0.0125$), all remain significant at $p < 0.001$.\nFeature--depth correlations (\\S\\ref{sec:results-differential}) are computed on class-balanced subsamples (equal samples per depth class) to control for depth-class imbalance in the generation pipeline.",
  "new_string": "\\paragraph{Metrics and statistical testing.}\nThe primary metric is \\textbf{macro-averaged F1} over the three depth classes.\nWe report 95\\% confidence intervals via percentile bootstrap ($n{=}1{,}000$), McNemar tests for pairwise comparisons, and paired bootstrap for baseline comparisons.\nAll $p$-values are two-sided; Bonferroni correction is applied where multiple comparisons arise.\nFeature--depth correlations (\\S\\ref{sec:results-differential}) use class-balanced subsamples to control for depth-class imbalance."
}

{
  "replace_all": false,

  "old_string": "\\paragraph{Length confound control.}\nSince TTR and hapax\\_ratio are mechanically length-dependent, we verify that depth signal is not an artifact of systematic length variation.\nWe conduct three complementary analyses: (1) length--depth correlation to verify absence of systematic length variation, (2) length--feature correlation to identify features with moderate length dependence, and (3) classification on length-matched subsets that equalize length distributions across depth classes (\\S\\ref{sec:results-classification}).",

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_018dg7fGYo8DhuNnrKAe7Wtx`</summary>

```text
1	To verify that the depth signal resides in the features rather than the classifier,
2	we repeat the 6-generator LOGO evaluation using Logistic Regression and Random Forest
3	in place of XGBoost (Table~\ref{tab:alt-classifier}).
4	All other settings (the same 7 features, 3-class labels, and class-balanced downsampling) are
5	kept identical. For Logistic Regression we apply standard scaling to the features.
6	
7	\begin{table}[h]
8	\centering
9	\caption{LOGO macro-F1 across three classifiers. Each column shows the held-out generator F1. (6-generator LOGO; 3-class.)}
10	\label{tab:alt-classifier}
11	\small
12	\resizebox{\linewidth}{!}{%
13	\begin{tabular}{lc cccccc}
14	\toprule
15	Classifier & Avg F1 & OLMo-2 & Py-1B & Py-2.8B & Qw-0.6B & Qw-1.7B & Qw-8B \\
16	\midrule
17	XGBoost          & 0.789 & 0.764 & 0.856 & 0.804 & 0.824 & 0.806 & 0.683 \\
18	Random Forest    & 0.790 & 0.764 & 0.856 & 0.804 & 0.825 & 0.807 & 0.684 \\
19	Logistic Regr.   & 0.725 & 0.719 & 0.781 & 0.740 & 0.745 & 0.743 & 0.624 \\
20	\bottomrule
21	\end{tabular}}
22	\end{table}
23	
24	Both tree-based classifiers achieve LOGO F1 $\approx 0.79$,
25	and even a linear model reaches $0.725$, well above the $0.33$ chance baseline for
26	three classes.
27	The per-generator ranking is also consistent across classifiers
28	(Pythia-1B easiest, Qwen3-8B hardest),
29	confirming that discriminative power originates in the entropy and lexical features,
30	not in classifier capacity.
31	
32	\subsection*{Sampling Sensitivity Analysis}
33	\label{app:sampling-sensitivity}
34	
35	To assess robustness to decoding hyperparameters, we regenerate Qwen3-1.7B continuation chains under three sampling configurations (the remaining five generators retain their default-configuration data) and re-evaluate the 6-generator LOGO pipeline (Table~\ref{tab:sampling-ablation}).
36	
37	\begin{table}[h]
38	\centering
39	\caption{Depth classification under varying sampling parameters. All other settings match the default pipeline. (6-generator LOGO; 3-class and binary.)}
40	\label{tab:sampling-ablation}
41	\small
42	\resizebox{\linewidth}{!}{%
43	\begin{tabular}{lcc ccc}
44	\toprule
45	Config & Temp.\ & Top-$p$ & 3-class F1 & Binary F1 & $\rho$(entropy\_mean) \\
46	\midrule
47	Low        & 0.5 & 0.80 & 0.785 & 0.797 & $-0.694$ \\
48	Default    & 0.7 & 0.90 & 0.788 & 0.799 & $-0.602$ \\
49	High       & 1.0 & 0.95 & 0.726 & 0.733 & $-0.002$ \\
50	\bottomrule
51	\end{tabular}}
52	\end{table}
53	
54	The depth signal is robust to moderate sampling variation: low and default configurations differ by at most 0.3\,pp in both 3-class and binary F1, and entropy--depth Spearman correlations remain strongly negative ($\rho \leq -0.60$).
55	At high temperature ($t{=}1.0$, top-$p{=}0.95$), classification degrades by approximately 6\,pp and the entropy--depth correlation collapses to near zero ($\rho = -0.002$).
56	This is consistent with the hypothesis that depth features are shaped by the sampling mechanism (\S\ref{sec:discussion}): elevated temperature injects sufficient randomness to flatten the distributional drift that accumulates across generation steps, effectively masking the depth signal.
57	The practical implication is that depth fingerprinting applies reliably under standard decoding regimes but should not be expected to generalize to high-entropy sampling settings.
58	
```

4	\centering
5	\small

16	\caption{Aggregated 3-class confusion matrix from 6-generator LOGO evaluation ($N=27{,}529$). Each cell shows count and row-normalized percentage. Gen-0 is near-perfectly identified. 17	\label{tab:confusion}
18	\end{table}

    "newString": "The practical implication is that depth fingerprinting applies reliably under standard decoding regimes but should not be expected to generalize to high-entropy sampling settings.\n\n\\subsection*{Per-Generator Performance}\n\nFigure~\\ref{fig:per-gen} visualizes per-generator LOGO performance.\nPythia models (1B, 2.8B) achieve highest F1, while larger models (Mistral-7B, Qwen3-8B) show degradation.\nThe scale effect (larger models $\\rightarrow$ lower F1) is consistent with higher-capacity models generating text statistically closer to human writing.\n\n\\begin{figure*}[ht]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{fig_appendix_per_gen.pdf}\n\\caption{Per-generator LOGO performance. (a) 8-generator binary and 3-class F1, sorted by binary F1. Dashed lines: pool average. (b) 6-generator LOGO detail heatmap showing the performance spread across model families.}\n\\label{fig:per-gen}\n\\end{figure*}",
    "originalFile": "To verify that the depth signal resides in the features rather than the classifier,\nwe repeat the 6-generator LOGO evaluation using Logistic Regression and Random Forest\nin place of XGBoost (Table~\\ref{tab:alt-classifier}).\nAll other settings (the same 7 features, 3-class labels, and class-balanced downsampling) are\nkept identical. For Logistic Regression we apply standard scaling to the features.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{LOGO macro-F1 across three classifiers. Each column shows the held-out generator F1. (6-generator LOGO; 3-class.)}\n\\label{tab:alt-classifier}\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\linewidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lc cccccc}\n\\toprule\nClassifier & Avg F1 & OLMo-2 & Py-1B & Py-2.8B & Qw-0.6B & Qw-1.7B & Qw-8B \\\\\n\\midrule\nXGBoost          & 0.789 & 0.764 & 0.856 & 0.804 & 0.824 & 0.806 & 0.683 \\\\\nRandom Forest    & 0.790 & 0.764 & 0.856 & 0.804 & 0.825 & 0.807 & 0.684 \\\\\nLogistic Regr.   & 0.725 & 0.719 & 0.781 & 0.740 & 0.745 & 0.743 & 0.624 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\nBoth tree-based classifiers achieve LOGO F1 $\\approx 0.79$,\nand even a linear model reaches $0.725$, well above the $0.33$ chance baseline for\nthree classes.\nThe per-generator ranking is also consistent across classifiers\n(Pythia-1B easiest, Qwen3-8B hardest),\nconfirming that discriminative power originates in the entropy and lexical features,\nnot in classifier capacity.\n\n\\subsection*{Sampling Sensitivity Analysis}\n\\label{app:sampling-sensitivity}\n\nTo assess robustness to decoding hyperparameters, we regenerate Qwen3-1.7B continuation chains under three sampling configurations (the remaining five generators retain their default-configuration data) and re-evaluate the 6-generator LOGO pipeline (Table~\\ref{tab:sampling-ablation}).\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Depth classification under varying sampling parameters. All other settings match the default pipeline. (6-generator LOGO; 3-class and binary.)}\n\\label{tab:sampling-ablation}\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\linewidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lcc ccc}\n\\toprule\nConfig & Temp.\\ & Top-$p$ & 3-class F1 & Binary F1 & $\\rho$(entropy\\_mean) \\\\\n\\midrule\nLow        & 0.5 & 0.80 & 0.785 & 0.797 & $-0.694$ \\\\\nDefault    & 0.7 & 0.90 & 0.788 & 0.799 & $-0.602$ \\\\\nHigh       & 1.0 & 0.95 & 0.726 & 0.733 & $-0.002$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe depth signal is robust to moderate sampling variation: low and default configurations differ by at most 0.3\\,pp in both 3-class and binary F1, and entropy--depth Spearman correlations remain strongly negative ($\\rho \\leq -0.60$).\nAt high temperature ($t{=}1.0$, top-$p{=}0.95$), classification degrades by approximately 6\\,pp and the entropy--depth correlation collapses to near zero ($\\rho = -0.002$).\nThis is consistent with the hypothesis that depth features are shaped by the sampling mechanism (\\S\\ref{sec:discussion}): elevated temperature injects sufficient randomness to flatten the distributional drift that accumulates across generation steps, effectively masking the depth signal.\nThe practical implication is that depth fingerprinting applies reliably under standard decoding regimes but should not be expected to generalize to high-entropy sampling settings.\n",

{
  "file_path": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_3/projects/gen-depth-fingerprint/artifacts/_project/paper/appendix_feature_distributions.tex",
  "content": "\\subsection*{DDR Trend Lines}\n\nFigure~\\ref{fig:ddr-trends} shows the per-feature depth trend (mean across all samples at each depth) with individual generator traces overlaid.\nThe consistent downward trajectories for drifting features across all generators confirm that DDR is a universal property of recursive generation.\nShape features exhibit flat trajectories regardless of generator, with near-zero $R^2$ for the linear fit.\n\n\\begin{figure*}[ht]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{fig_appendix_ddr_trends.pdf}\n\\caption{Per-feature depth trends. Black line: pooled mean ($\\pm$1 std shading) across all generators. Colored traces: individual generators. Features are labeled by DDR category (drifting, intermediate, stationary). Spearman $\\rho$ and linear $R^2$ are annotated per panel.}\n\\label{fig:ddr-trends}\n\\end{figure*}\n\n\\subsection*{UMAP Embedding}\n\nFigure~\\ref{fig:umap} shows a 2D UMAP projection of the 7-feature space (7{,}500 stratified samples, StandardScaler normalization, $k$\\,=\\,30 neighbors, min\\_dist\\,=\\,0.3).\nPanel (a) reveals a depth gradient from gen-0 through gen-4, with progressive overlap at deeper generations consistent with the convergence that motivates the gen-2+ class collapse.\nPanel (b) shows the 3-class view: gen-0 forms a separable cluster, while gen-1 and gen-2+ overlap substantially, explaining the classification difficulty at this boundary.\n\n\\begin{figure*}[ht]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{fig_appendix_umap.pdf}\n\\caption{UMAP 2D projection of the 7-feature space ($N$\\,=\\,7{,}500 stratified sample). (a) Colored by raw generation depth (0--4): a gradient structure emerges, with deeper depths converging. (b) Colored by 3-class label: gen-0 separates clearly; gen-1 and gen-2+ overlap, consistent with the high false-negative rate at the gen-1/gen-2+ boundary.}\n\\label{fig:umap}\n\\end{figure*}\n"
}

          "-\\begin{figure*}[ht]",
          "-\\centering",
          "-\\includegraphics[width=0.85\\textwidth]{fig_appendix_confusion.pdf}",
          "-\\caption{3-class LOGO confusion matrix ($N$\\,=\\,27{,}529). (a) Raw counts. (b) Row-normalized recall percentages. Gen-0 achieves near-perfect classification; the primary confusion axis is gen-1 $\\leftrightarrow$ gen-2+.}",
          "-\\label{fig:confusion-heatmap}",
          "-\\end{figure*}",
          "-",
          "-\\subsection*{Per-Generator Performance}",

          "-Panel (a) shows the 8-generator ranking: Pythia models (1B, 2.8B) achieve highest F1, while larger models (Mistral-7B, Qwen3-8B) show degradation.",
          "-Dashed lines indicate pool-average performance.",
          "-The scale effect (larger models $\\rightarrow$ lower F1) is consistent with higher-capacity models generating text statistically closer to human writing.",
          "-",

          "-\\centering",

          "-\\caption{Per-generator LOGO performance. (a) 8-generator binary and 3-class F1, sorted by binary F1. Dashed lines: pool average. (b) 6-generator LOGO detail heatmap showing the performance spread across model families.}",
          "-\\label{fig:per-gen}",

    "originalFile": "This appendix provides detailed visualizations of the feature distributions across generation depths and the resulting embedding structure.\n\n\\subsection*{Per-Feature Violin Plots}\n\nFigure~\\ref{fig:violin} shows the full distribution of each feature across five generation depths (0--4), pooled across all eight generators ($N$\\,=\\,37{,}058).\nThe violin plots confirm the DDR pattern identified in the main text: drifting features (entropy mean, TTR, hapax ratio) show progressive narrowing and location shift, while shape features (entropy max, tail decay) maintain approximately constant distributions across depths.\nEntropy std exhibits intermediate behavior with modest location shift.\n\n\\begin{figure*}[ht]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{fig_appendix_violin.pdf}\n\\caption{Feature distributions by generation depth (violin plots, $N$\\,=\\,37{,}058). Drifting features (entropy mean, TTR, hapax ratio: $|\\rho| > 0.60$) show clear location shifts and narrowing with increasing depth. Shape features (entropy max, tail decay: $|\\rho| < 0.10$) remain approximately stationary. Quartile lines within each violin indicate median and interquartile range.}\n\\label{fig:violin}\n\\end{figure*}\n\n\\subsection*{DDR Trend Lines}\n\nFigure~\\ref{fig:ddr-trends} shows the per-feature depth trend (mean across all samples at each depth) with individual generator traces overlaid.\nThe consistent downward trajectories for drifting features across all generators confirm that DDR is a universal property of recursive generation, not specific to any single model.\nShape features exhibit flat trajectories regardless of generator, with near-zero $R^2$ for the linear fit.\n\n\\begin{figure*}[ht]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{fig_appendix_ddr_trends.pdf}\n\\caption{Per-feature depth trends. Black line: pooled mean ($\\pm$1 std shading) across all generators. Colored traces: individual generators. Features are labeled by DDR category (drifting, intermediate, stationary). Spearman $\\rho$ and linear $R^2$ are annotated per panel.}\n\\label{fig:ddr-trends}\n\\end{figure*}\n\n\\subsection*{UMAP Embedding}\n\nFigure~\\ref{fig:umap} shows a 2D UMAP projection of the 7-feature space (7{,}500 stratified samples, StandardScaler normalization, $k$\\,=\\,30 neighbors, min\\_dist\\,=\\,0.3).\nPanel (a) reveals a depth gradient from gen-0 (green) through gen-4 (yellow), with progressive overlap at deeper generations consistent with the convergence that motivates the gen-2+ class collapse.\nPanel (b) shows the 3-class view: gen-0 forms a separable cluster, while gen-1 and gen-2+ overlap substantially in feature space, explaining the classification difficulty at this boundary.\n\n\\begin{figure*}[ht]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{fig_appendix_umap.pdf}\n\\caption{UMAP 2D projection of the 7-feature space ($N$\\,=\\,7{,}500 stratified sample). (a) Colored by raw generation depth (0--4): a gradient structure emerges, with deeper depths converging. (b) Colored by 3-class label: gen-0 separates clearly; gen-1 and gen-2+ overlap, consistent with the high false-negative rate at the gen-1/gen-2+ boundary.}\n\\label{fig:umap}\n\\end{figure*}\n\n\\subsection*{Confusion Matrix Visualization}\n\nFigure~\\ref{fig:confusion-heatmap} provides a heatmap visualization of the 3-class LOGO confusion matrix (complementing Table~\\ref{tab:confusion} in Appendix~\\ref{app:confusion}).\nThe row-normalized view in panel (b) highlights: (1) near-perfect gen-0 classification (99.4\\% recall), (2) gen-1 confusion primarily with gen-2+ (18.8\\%) rather than gen-0 (6.2\\%), and (3) gen-2+ misclassification predominantly into gen-1 (23.3\\%), consistent with DDR predictions that shallow recursive text has weak fingerprints.\n\n\\begin{figure*}[ht]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=0.85\\textwidth]{fig_appendix_confusion.pdf}\n\\caption{3-class LOGO confusion matrix ($N$\\,=\\,27{,}529). (a) Raw counts. (b) Row-normalized recall percentages. Gen-0 achieves near-perfect classification; the primary confusion axis is gen-1 $\\leftrightarrow$ gen-2+.}\n\\label{fig:confusion-heatmap}\n\\end{figure*}\n\n\\subsection*{Per-Generator Performance}\n\nFigure~\\ref{fig:per-gen} visualizes per-generator LOGO performance.\nPanel (a) shows the 8-generator ranking: Pythia models (1B, 2.8B) achieve highest F1, while larger models (Mistral-7B, Qwen3-8B) show degradation.\nDashed lines indicate pool-average performance.\nThe scale effect (larger models $\\rightarrow$ lower F1) is consistent with higher-capacity models generating text statistically closer to human writing.\n\n\\begin{figure*}[ht]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{fig_appendix_per_gen.pdf}\n\\caption{Per-generator LOGO performance. (a) 8-generator binary and 3-class F1, sorted by binary F1. Dashed lines: pool average. (b) 6-generator LOGO detail heatmap showing the performance spread across model families.}\n\\label{fig:per-gen}\n\\end{figure*}\n",
    "userModified": false
  },

{
  "description": "Redesign framework figure",
  "prompt": "Create a professional academic framework figure for a paper about \"Depth Fingerprinting\" — detecting the generation depth of LLM-generated text. The current figure at `/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_3/projects/gen-depth-fingerprint/artifacts/_project/chart/fig2_framework.pdf` is too simple (just 5 boxes in a row). The user wants something more professional, like published NLP/ML papers.\n\n**What the method does**:\n1. **Data construction**: Human seed text (gen-0) is fed through an LLM to produce gen-1, which is fed again to produce gen-2, etc. This creates a recursive chain of increasing \"depth\".\n2. **Feature extraction**: For each text, compute 7 features in 2 groups:\n   - Reference-model features (using Pythia-410M): entropy_mean, entropy_std, entropy_min, entropy_max, tail_decay (from token-level NLL distribution)\n   - Surface features: TTR (type-token ratio), hapax_ratio\n3. **DDR Decomposition**: Features split into:\n   - Drifting features (|ρ| > 0.60): entropy_mean, TTR, hapax_ratio — these change monotonically with depth\n   - Stationary features (|ρ| < 0.10): entropy_max, tail_decay — these stay stable\n   - Intermediate: entropy_std, entropy_min\n4. **Classification**: XGBoost with LOGO (Leave-One-Generator-Out) cross-validation → output: depth d ∈ {0, 1, 2+}\n\n**Design requirements**:\n- The figure should have a clear flow from LEFT to RIGHT\n- Use THREE main sections/panels:\n  - **Left**: Show the recursive generation chain as a pipeline (text₀ → G₁ → text₁ → G₂ → text₂ → ...) with generation depth labels. Maybe show a small text snippet getting transformed.\n  - **Middle**: Feature extraction showing a small NLL distribution curve (token-level surprise) and the 7 features being computed. Show the DDR split visually (drifting features with downward arrows, stationary features with flat arrows or a horizontal line).\n  - **Right**: Classification pipeline (XGBoost → 3-class prediction)\n- Use a professional color scheme (muted blues, greens, oranges — not garish)\n- Use matplotlib with clean academic styling\n- Output as PDF at `/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_3/projects/gen-depth-fingerprint/artifacts/_project/chart/fig2_framework.pdf`\n- The figure will be used at `\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]` in a 2-column ACL paper (figure* environment, so full text width ~6.5 inches)\n- Figure height should be reasonable (about 2-2.5 inches) — not too tall\n- Use matplotlib's `plt.savefig` with tight bbox and high dpi (300+)\n- Font size should be readable at print size (minimum 7pt, ideally 8-9pt)\n- Do NOT use LaTeX rendering in matplotlib (no `usetex=True`) — use regular matplotlib text\n\n**Style reference**: Look at framework/overview figures in NLP papers like FastDetectGPT, Binoculars, or any ACL/EMNLP paper. They typically use:\n- Rounded rectangles with subtle fill colors\n- Thin connecting arrows\n- Small inset visualizations (mini plots, distribution curves)\n- Clean typography with sans-serif fonts\n- Grouped sub-components with labeled regions\n\nCreate the Python script and run it to generate the PDF. Also generate a PNG version for preview.\n",
  "run_in_background": true
}

The user wants something more professional, like published NLP/ML papers.\n\n**What the method does**:\n1. **Data construction**: Human seed text (gen-0) is fed through an LLM to produce gen-1, which is fed again to produce gen-2, etc. This creates a recursive chain of increasing \"depth\".\n2. **Feature extraction**: For each text, compute 7 features in 2 groups:\n   - Reference-model features (using Pythia-410M): entropy_mean, entropy_std, entropy_min, entropy_max, tail_decay (from token-level NLL distribution)\n   - Surface features: TTR (type-token ratio), hapax_ratio\n3. **DDR Decomposition**: Features split into:\n   - Drifting features (|ρ| > 0.60): entropy_mean, TTR, hapax_ratio — these change monotonically with depth\n   - Stationary features (|ρ| < 0.10): entropy_max, tail_decay — these stay stable\n   - Intermediate: entropy_std, entropy_min\n4. **Classification**: XGBoost with LOGO (Leave-One-Generator-Out) cross-validation → output: depth d ∈ {0, 1, 2+}\n\n**Design requirements**:\n- The figure should have a clear flow from LEFT to RIGHT\n- Use THREE main sections/panels:\n  - **Left**: Show the recursive generation chain as a pipeline (text₀ → G₁ → text₁ → G₂ → text₂ → ...) with generation depth labels. Maybe show a small text snippet getting transformed.\n  - **Middle**: Feature extraction showing a small NLL distribution curve (token-level surprise) and the 7 features being computed. Show the DDR split visually (drifting features with downward arrows, stationary features with flat arrows or a horizontal line).\n  - **Right**: Classification pipeline (XGBoost → 3-class prediction)\n- Use a professional color scheme (muted blues, greens, oranges — not garish)\n- Use matplotlib with clean academic styling\n- Output as PDF at `/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_3/projects/gen-depth-fingerprint/artifacts/_project/chart/fig2_framework.pdf`\n- The figure will be used at `\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]` in a 2-column ACL paper (figure* environment, so full text width ~6.5 inches)\n- Figure height should be reasonable (about 2-2.5 inches) — not too tall\n- Use matplotlib's `plt.savefig` with tight bbox and high dpi (300+)\n- Font size should be readable at print size (minimum 7pt, ideally 8-9pt)\n- Do NOT use LaTeX rendering in matplotlib (no `usetex=True`) — use regular matplotlib text\n\n**Style reference**: Look at framework/overview figures in NLP papers like FastDetectGPT, Binoculars, or any ACL/EMNLP paper. They typically use:\n- Rounded rectangles with subtle fill colors\n- Thin connecting arrows\n- Small inset visualizations (mini plots, distribution curves)\n- Clean typography with sans-serif fonts\n- Grouped sub-components with labeled regions\n\nCreate the Python script and run it to generate the PDF. Also generate a PNG version for preview.\n",

    "oldString": "confirming that discriminative power originates in the entropy and lexical features,\nnot in classifier capacity.",
    "newString": "confirming that discriminative power originates in the entropy and lexical features,\nnot in classifier capacity.\nOrdinal-specific methods (CORAL \\cite{coral_ordinal}, Frank-Hall \\cite{frank_hall_ordinal}) achieve comparable F1 with overlapping CIs, confirming that 3-class granularity provides insufficient resolution for ranking-aware losses to improve over standard multiclass classification.",
    "originalFile": "To verify that the depth signal resides in the features rather than the classifier,\nwe repeat the 6-generator LOGO evaluation using Logistic Regression and Random Forest\nin place of XGBoost (Table~\\ref{tab:alt-classifier}).\nAll other settings (the same 7 features, 3-class labels, and class-balanced downsampling) are\nkept identical. For Logistic Regression we apply standard scaling to the features.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{LOGO macro-F1 across three classifiers. Each column shows the held-out generator F1. (6-generator LOGO; 3-class.)}\n\\label{tab:alt-classifier}\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\linewidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lc cccccc}\n\\toprule\nClassifier & Avg F1 & OLMo-2 & Py-1B & Py-2.8B & Qw-0.6B & Qw-1.7B & Qw-8B \\\\\n\\midrule\nXGBoost          & 0.789 & 0.764 & 0.856 & 0.804 & 0.824 & 0.806 & 0.683 \\\\\nRandom Forest    & 0.790 & 0.764 & 0.856 & 0.804 & 0.825 & 0.807 & 0.684 \\\\\nLogistic Regr.   & 0.725 & 0.719 & 0.781 & 0.740 & 0.745 & 0.743 & 0.624 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\nBoth tree-based classifiers achieve LOGO F1 $\\approx 0.79$,\nand even a linear model reaches $0.725$, well above the $0.33$ chance baseline for\nthree classes.\nThe per-generator ranking is also consistent across classifiers\n(Pythia-1B easiest, Qwen3-8B hardest),\nconfirming that discriminative power originates in the entropy and lexical features,\nnot in classifier capacity.\n\n\\subsection*{Sampling Sensitivity Analysis}\n\\label{app:sampling-sensitivity}\n\nTo assess robustness to decoding hyperparameters, we regenerate Qwen3-1.7B continuation chains under three sampling configurations (the remaining five generators retain their default-configuration data) and re-evaluate the 6-generator LOGO pipeline (Table~\\ref{tab:sampling-ablation}).\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Depth classification under varying sampling parameters. All other settings match the default pipeline. (6-generator LOGO; 3-class and binary.)}\n\\label{tab:sampling-ablation}\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\linewidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lcc ccc}\n\\toprule\nConfig & Temp.\\ & Top-$p$ & 3-class F1 & Binary F1 & $\\rho$(entropy\\_mean) \\\\\n\\midrule\nLow        & 0.5 & 0.80 & 0.785 & 0.797 & $-0.694$ \\\\\nDefault    & 0.7 & 0.90 & 0.788 & 0.799 & $-0.602$ \\\\\nHigh       & 1.0 & 0.95 & 0.726 & 0.733 & $-0.002$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe depth signal is robust to moderate sampling variation: low and default configurations differ by at most 0.3\\,pp in both 3-class and binary F1, and entropy--depth Spearman correlations remain strongly negative ($\\rho \\leq -0.60$).\nAt high temperature ($t{=}1.0$, top-$p{=}0.95$), classification degrades by approximately 6\\,pp and the entropy--depth correlation collapses to near zero ($\\rho = -0.002$).\nThis is consistent with the hypothesis that depth features are shaped by the sampling mechanism (\\S\\ref{sec:discussion}): elevated temperature injects sufficient randomness to flatten the distributional drift that accumulates across generation steps, effectively masking the depth signal.\nThe practical implication is that depth fingerprinting applies reliably under standard decoding regimes but should not be expected to generalize to high-entropy sampling settings.\n\n\\subsection*{Per-Generator Performance}\n\nFigure~\\ref{fig:per-gen} visualizes per-generator LOGO performance.\nPythia models (1B, 2.8B) achieve highest F1, while larger models (Mistral-7B, Qwen3-8B) show degradation.\nThe scale effect (larger models $\\rightarrow$ lower F1) is consistent with higher-capacity models generating text statistically closer to human writing.\n\n\\begin{figure*}[ht]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{fig_appendix_per_gen.pdf}\n\\caption{Per-generator LOGO performance. (a) 8-generator binary and 3-class F1, sorted by binary F1. Dashed lines: pool average. (b) 6-generator LOGO detail heatmap showing the performance spread across model families.}\n\\label{fig:per-gen}\n\\end{figure*}\n",

Also use for: permissions (\"allow X\", \"add permission\", \"move permission to\"), env vars (\"set X=Y\"), hook troubleshooting, or any changes to settings.json/settings.local.json files. For simple settings like theme/model, suggest the /config command.\n- keybindings-help: Use when the user wants to customize keyboard shortcuts, rebind keys, add chord bindings, or modify ~/.claude/keybindings.json. Examples: \"rebind ctrl+s\", \"add a chord shortcut\", \"change the submit key\", \"customize keybindings\".\n- simplify: Review changed code for reuse, quality, and efficiency, then fix any issues found.\n- fewer-permission-prompts: Scan your transcripts for common read-only Bash and MCP tool calls, then add a prioritized allowlist to project .claude/settings.json to reduce permission prompts.\n- loop: Run a prompt or slash command on a recurring interval (e.g. /loop 5m /foo).
